At a finite `x` other than 0 and an `n` other than 0, `pown_dir_nonzero` shows that it returns `pown_pos` at `|x|`, negated and in the other direction for `x < 0` and an odd `n`. `pown_pos` takes the exponent `g` of `x`, with `2^g <= x < 2^(g+1)`, and settles the arguments at which `g n` and `(g + 1) n` show that `x^n >= 2^1024` or `x^n <= 2^-1075` by `pown_rule_overflow` and `pown_rule_underflow`, at which no finite `f64` is `x^n` (`pown_rule_not_exact`). `pown_pos_post` shows that it returns, where it returns, a value with the property `post` in its direction, by these rules, by the fast path at `y = n`, where |n ln x| >= 2^-54 for `x` other than 1 (`pown_fast_hyp`), and, at the other arguments, by the driver, from level 1 or 2 by a threshold on `|n|` that no proof depends on, and the enclosures that `ev_pown_correct` shows hold `x^n` at each level: `ev_pown` raises the point `x` to the power `|n|` by binary powering with `encl_mul`, and divides 1 by the result with `encl_div` for `n < 0`. `powi_spec` shows that binary powering encloses the power of every point of its argument, bounds the exponents of its result, and gives the point `x^p` itself where its argument is the point `x` and every `x^j`, `j` from 1 to `p`, has at most `64 n` significant bits at level `n`. `pow_dir_exact` shows that `pow` returns `v` in both directions at every finite `x > 0` and finite `y` at which x^y is a finite `f64` `v`, from `pow_rule_not_exact` and `exact_complete`, or, where the fast path comes first and returns, from `fast_pow_dir_exact`, that it then returns `v`, and `pown_dir_exact` the same of `pown` at every finite `x` other than 0 and every `n` with `is_i32 n` at which `x^n` is a finite `f64` `v`, from `pown_dir_nonzero` and `pown_pos_exact`. That rests on `pown_rule_not_exact`, on `ev_pown_exact`, which shows from `pown_exact_odd` (a finite `x > 0` whose `x^n` is a finite `f64` is `m 2^e` with `m > 0` and `m^|n| < 2^53`, and `m = 1` for `n < 0`) and `powi_spec` that `ev_pown` gives `[v, v]` at every level, and on `ziv_exact`, which shows that `ziv` returns a finite `v` other than 0, from a `start` of at most 64, where every level gives `[v, v]` (`driver_exact`). Where the fast path returns before the loop, it returns the rounding of the `f64` `x^n`, which is `x^n` itself (`fast_pow_dir_correct`). Both theorems are about the Rust model, `pow_dir` and `pown_dir` with `Dyadic.ziv`. They come from `pow_dir_exact_with`, which holds for every driver, as `exact` or the fast path settles these arguments before the loop, and from `pown_dir_exact_with`, which holds for every driver `z` with `driver_exact z`, as `ziv_strict` also has (`ziv_strict_exact`). `DyPoint.v` takes both at `ziv_strict`. These two theorems say nothing of the other arguments:

```coq
Theorem pow_dir_correct :
  forall x y (up : bool) v,
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite y = true -> pow_dom (B2R x) (B2R y) ->
  DyadicPow.pow_dir_with ziv_strict x y up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (Rpow (B2R x) (B2R y)) (with_val v).

Theorem pow_dir_exact :
  forall x y (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> (0 < B2R x)%R -> is_finite y = true -> is_finite v = true ->
  Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y) = B2R v ->
  DyadicPow.pow_dir x y up = Some v.

Theorem pown_dir_correct :
  forall x n (up : bool) v,
  is_i32 n -> is_finite x = true -> pown_dom n (B2R x) ->
  DyadicPow.pown_dir_with ziv_strict x n up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (powerRZ (B2R x) n) (with_val v).

Theorem pown_dir_exact :
  forall x n (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_i32 n -> is_finite x = true -> B2R x <> 0%R -> is_finite v = true ->
  powerRZ (B2R x) n = B2R v ->
  DyadicPow.pown_dir x n up = Some v.
```

The fast path of `pow` and `pow_near`, after their rules and before the integer `y` that go to x^n, `pow_near`'s before `exact_near` and `pow`'s before `exact` but where `maybe_exact` says that x^y may be an `f64`, and of `pown_pos` and `pown_pos_near`, after their two rules, at `y = n`, before `ziv` and `ziv_near`, is `fast_pow_dir(x, y, up)` and `fast_pow_near(x, y)` of `src/dyadic/pow.rs`, for a finite `x > 0`, which `pow` and `pow_near` reach through `fast_pow_dir_call` and `fast_pow_near_call`, which only call them and are kept out of line. They take t = y ln x as `th + tl` from `pow_t(x, y)`, and e^t 2^-e, for an integer `e`, as an unevaluated sum `h + l` of two `f64`s from `fast_exp(th, tl)` above, which they round with the tests `round_dir_err` and `round_near_err` of `src/dyadic/fast.rs` at 2^-67 (`ERR67`), or return `None` where these fail. Where `fast_exp` returns `None`, which it does unless `th` is in [-707, 709], they call `fast_pow_wide_dir` and `fast_pow_wide_near` instead (below). `pow_t` takes ln x from `ln_acc(x)` of `src/dyadic/log.rs`, which computes it as `fast_ln` does, from `ln_split` and the table `LOG_TABLE`, but with `ln_core_acc` and `log1p_poly_acc` in place of `ln_core` and `log1p_poly`, and without the normalization: `log1p_poly_acc` takes the Taylor polynomial of ln(1 + z) of degree 9 at `z = z1 + z2`, with `z1 - z1^2/2 + z1^3/3` in double-double and the terms of degree 4 to 9 by Horner's scheme in `f64`. `log1p_shift2` in [`FastPowTaylor.v`](theories/FastPowTaylor.v) bounds the terms of `z2` that it leaves out, `log1p_horner4_correct` in [`FastPowHorner.v`](theories/FastPowHorner.v) the round-off of its Horner's scheme, by 13/10 2^-53 |z1|^4 + 2^-1073, `log1p_cube_correct` in [`FastPowCube.v`](theories/FastPowCube.v) that of `z1^3/3`, and `log1p_poly_acc_correct` in [`FastPowPoly.v`](theories/FastPowPoly.v) the error of `log1p_poly_acc`, with `log1p_taylor` of `FastLogTaylor.v` at degree 9. `ln_core_acc_correct` in [`FastPowCore.v`](theories/FastPowCore.v) shows from these, the table and the exact reduction of `FastLogReduce.v` that `ln_core_acc` gives `ln x - e ln 2` within 2^-79 of it, relative, and `ln_acc_correct` in [`FastPowLn.v`](theories/FastPowLn.v) that `ln_acc` adds `e ln 2` and gives ln x as `lh + ll` within 17/16 2^-79 |ln x|. `pow_t` then gives `t` as `th + tl`, with `th = RN(y lh)` and `tl` the error of that product (`two_prod_err`) plus `RN(y ll)`. `fast_pow_mul` in [`FastPowMul.v`](theories/FastPowMul.v) shows that, where |y ln x| >= 2^-60 and |th| <= 709, |tl| <= 2^-40, as `fast_exp` needs, `th + tl` is within 3/4 2^-69 of y ln x, and the error of the product is the same with and without an FMA, `fast_pow_mul_wide` the same where |th| <= 752, with |tl| <= 3 2^-42, as `fast_exp_edge` needs, and `th + tl` within 13/16 2^-69 of y ln x, and `fast_pow_mul_one` that `pow_t` returns two zeros at x = 1 and |y| <= 2^900. A change d of the argument moves e^z 2^-e, which is below 2 + 2^-68, by at most (2 + 2^-68) d (1 + 2d), so that `fast_pow_correct` in [`FastPow.v`](theories/FastPow.v) shows that x^y 2^-e is within 2^-67 of the `h + l` of `fast_exp`, from `fast_exp_correct`. `fast_pow_wide_dir` and `fast_pow_wide_near` return the overflow, `overflow(up)` and +∞, where `th` >= `T_OVER` = 6243315534363256 2^-43, the `f64` nearest to 709.7828, which is above 1024 ln 2 = 709.78271..., and the underflow, `underflow(up)` and +0, where `th` <= `T_UNDER` = -746: `fast_pow_overflow` in [`FastPowRules.v`](theories/FastPowRules.v) shows that x^y > 2^1024 in the first case, and `fast_pow_underflow` that 0 < x^y < 2^-1075 in the second, as a finite `th` of magnitude at least 1 is y ln x within 2^-51 |th|, from `ln_acc_correct` and |ln x| >= 2^-54 at an `f64` x other than 1 (`ln_f64_ne1` of `FastLogNorm.v`), and an infinite one has the sign of y ln x, with |y ln x| >= 1024. Between them, they take e^t 2^-e as `h + l` from `fast_exp_edge(th, tl)`, as for e^x, and round it with `round_dir_wide_err` and `round_near_wide_err` of `src/dyadic/fast.rs`, above, at 2^-67, subnormal results included. These return `None` for an `e` above 1023, so that the x^y at which `fast_exp_edge` returns such an `e` go to `ziv` and `ziv_near`. `fast_pow_edge_correct` shows, from `fast_exp_edge_correct` and `fast_pow_mul_wide`, that x^y 2^-e is within 2^-67 of that `h + l`, with `e` in [-1085, 1026], where |y ln x| >= 2^-60 and 2^9 <= |th| <= 752. `fast_pow_dir_correct` and `fast_pow_near_correct` show that the fast path returns, where it returns, x^y rounded in its direction, at every finite `x > 0` and finite `y` with |y ln x| >= 2^-60, or with x = 1 and |y| <= 2^900. These include every argument at which `pow` calls it, as |y ln x| >= 2^-57 there by the rules, and every one at which `pown_pos` does, as |n ln x| >= 2^-54 at an `f64` x other than 1 (`pown_fast_hyp` in `DyPown.v`). At x = 1, which only `pown_pos` passes, the paths rounded in a direction return `None` and those rounded to nearest return 1. Where it returns at an x^y or an x^n that is an `f64`, it returns that `f64` (`fast_pow_dir_exact` in `DyPow.v` for `pow`). Where it returns `None`, `pow` calls `exact`, unless `maybe_exact` sent x^y there first, and `pow_near` calls `exact_near`, which settles the x^y that are `f64`s and, rounded to nearest, the midpoints. That the tests fail at the x^y that are `f64`s in a direction and at the midpoints to nearest, which is why `exact` and `exact_near` still run there, is checked by reading `src/dyadic/fast.rs`, not proved: no proof needs it. As for e^x, `log1p_poly_acc`, `ln_core_acc` and `pow_t` compute the errors of their exact products with `two_prod_err::<HAS_FMA>`, the theorems hold for either value of `fma`, and `fast_pow_dir_fma` and `fast_pow_near_fma` show that both give the same result, so that `DyadicPow.pow_dir_with` and the others pass `false`:

```coq
Theorem ln_acc_correct :
  forall fma x, is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x ->
  let '(lh, ll) := DyadicLog.ln_acc fma x in
  is_finite lh = true /\ is_finite ll = true /\ B2R lh = RN (B2R lh + B2R ll) /\
  Rabs (B2R lh) <= 745 /\
  Rabs (ln (B2R x) - (B2R lh + B2R ll)) <= 17 / 16 * bpow radix2 (-79) * Rabs (ln (B2R x)).

Theorem fast_pow_mul :
  forall fma x y,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x)) ->
  let '(lh, ll) := DyadicLog.ln_acc fma x in
  let th := mul y lh in
  is_finite th = true -> Rabs (B2R th) <= 709 ->
  let tl := add (Generic.two_prod_err fma y lh th) (mul y ll) in
  is_finite tl = true /\ Rabs (B2R tl) <= bpow radix2 (-40) /\
  Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x) - (B2R th + B2R tl)) <= 3 / 4 * bpow radix2 (-69) /\
  Generic.two_prod_err true y lh th = Generic.two_prod_err false y lh th.

Theorem fast_pow_mul_wide :
  forall fma x y,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x)) ->
  let '(lh, ll) := DyadicLog.ln_acc fma x in
  let th := mul y lh in
  is_finite th = true -> Rabs (B2R th) <= 752 ->
  let tl := add (Generic.two_prod_err fma y lh th) (mul y ll) in
  is_finite tl = true /\ Rabs (B2R tl) <= 3 * bpow radix2 (-42) /\
  Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x) - (B2R th + B2R tl)) <= 13 / 16 * bpow radix2 (-69) /\
  Generic.two_prod_err true y lh th = Generic.two_prod_err false y lh th.

Theorem fast_pow_correct :
  forall fma x y th tl h l e,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  ((B2R x = 1 /\ Rabs (B2R y) <= bpow radix2 900) \/
   bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x))) ->
  DyadicPow.pow_t fma x y = (th, tl) ->
  DyadicExp.fast_exp fma th tl = Some (h, l, e) ->
  is_finite h = true /\ is_finite l = true /\ B2R h = RN (B2R h + B2R l) /\
  / 2 < B2R h < 2 /\ (-1021 <= e <= 1022)%Z /\
  Rabs (Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y) * bpow radix2 (- e) - (B2R h + B2R l)) <= bpow radix2 (-67).

Theorem fast_pow_edge_correct :
  forall fma x y th tl h l e,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x)) ->
  DyadicPow.pow_t fma x y = (th, tl) ->
  is_finite th = true -> bpow radix2 9 <= Rabs (B2R th) <= 752 ->
  DyadicExp.fast_exp_edge fma th tl = Some (h, l, e) ->
  is_finite h = true /\ is_finite l = true /\ B2R h = RN (B2R h + B2R l) /\
  / 2 < B2R h < 2 /\ (-1085 <= e <= 1026)%Z /\
  Rabs (Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y) * bpow radix2 (- e) - (B2R h + B2R l)) <= bpow radix2 (-67).

Theorem fast_pow_overflow :
  forall fma x y th tl,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  DyadicPow.pow_t fma x y = (th, tl) -> ge th DyadicPow.t_over = true ->
  bpow radix2 1024 < Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y).

Theorem fast_pow_dir_correct :
  forall fma x y up v,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  ((B2R x = 1 /\ Rabs (B2R y) <= bpow radix2 900) \/
   bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x))) ->
  DyadicPow.fast_pow_dir fma x y up = Some v ->
  rounds up (Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y)) v.

Theorem fast_pow_dir_fma :
  forall x y up,
  is_finite x = true -> 0 < B2R x -> is_finite y = true ->
  ((B2R x = 1 /\ Rabs (B2R y) <= bpow radix2 900) \/
   bpow radix2 (-60) <= Rabs (B2R y * ln (B2R x))) ->
  DyadicPow.fast_pow_dir true x y up = DyadicPow.fast_pow_dir false x y up.
```

The other 19 functions of the kernel that call `ziv` return their exact results too. `exp_dir_exact` and the others of [`DyExpExact.v`](theories/DyExpExact.v), [`DyLogExact.v`](theories/DyLogExact.v), [`DyAtanExact.v`](theories/DyAtanExact.v) and [`DyTrigExact.v`](theories/DyTrigExact.v), one `<f>_dir_exact` for each of `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `ln`, `log2`, `log10`, `asinh`, `acosh`, `atanh`, `atan`, `asin`, `acos`, `atan2`, `sin`, `cos` and `tan`, show that it returns `v` in both directions at every finite argument at which its exact result is a finite `f64` `v`, in the domain of its `_correct` theorem above (`log_dom` for the logarithms, and `acosh_dom`, `atanh_dom`, `asin_dom` and `acos_dom`) or, for `atan2`, in `atan2_dom` of `Elementary.v`, the domain of IEEE 1788, which also has `y = ±0` with `x < 0`, where the result ±π is not an `f64`. Where the result is 0, each says which zero it returns: `x`, the zero of the same sign, for `sinh`, `tanh`, `asinh`, `atanh`, `atan`, `asin`, `sin` and `tan`, `y` for `atan2`, and `zero`, the `f64` +0, for `ln`, `log2`, `log10`, `acosh` and `acos`, while the results of `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10`, `cosh` and `cos` are never 0. Those of `sinh`, `cosh`, `asinh` and `acosh` take as a premise `exp_not_quadratic` of [`ExpQuadratic.v`](theories/ExpQuadratic.v): for every rational r other than 0, e^r is not a root of a nonzero polynomial of degree at most 2 with integer coefficients. It follows from the theorem of Hermite and Lindemann, that e^r is transcendental for every algebraic r other than 0, which is not proved here. It is a definition, not an axiom, so `Print Assumptions` does not list it, and the other 15 theorems hold without it. An `f64` is a rational (`f64_rational` in [`DyExactDriver.v`](theories/DyExactDriver.v)), and the proofs reduce the other 15 functions to three facts about rationals r = p/q, proved here, and those four to `exp_not_quadratic`. `exp_irrational` in [`ExpIrrational.v`](theories/ExpIrrational.v) shows that e^r is irrational for every rational r other than 0, by Niven's method as for `pi_irrational` above, with x^n (1 - x)^n of `NivenSym.v`, first for an integer r > 0. `cos_irrational` in [`CosIrrational.v`](theories/CosIrrational.v) shows the same of cos r, by Niven's proof ("Irrational Numbers", 1956, ch. 2) with the polynomial x^(p-1) (a - bx)^(2p) (2a - bx)^(p-1) of [`CosNiven.v`](theories/CosNiven.v) for r = a/b and cos r = c/d, but with the odd p = 2adK + 1, which is prime to 2a and to d, for a large integer K in place of a large prime p. `pow2_rational` and `pow10_rational` in [`PowFactor.v`](theories/PowFactor.v) show that 2^r and 10^r are rational only where q divides p, from the exponents of 2 in a^q = (2o)^p b^q, for 2o = 2 or 10, an odd o and (2o)^r = a/b with p >= 0, and likewise with a and b swapped for p < 0. With them, e^x and tanh x are `f64`s only at x = ±0, as e^(2x) = (1 + t)/(1 - t) for t = tanh x, and ln x and atanh x only where they are 0, as e^s = x and e^(2s) = (1 + x)/(1 - x) for their results s; under `exp_not_quadratic`, sinh x and cosh x only at x = ±0, and asinh x and acosh x only where they are 0, as (e^s)^2 - 2 t e^s ∓ 1 = 0 for s = x and t = sinh x or cosh x, or t = x and s = asinh x or acosh x; 2^x and 10^x only at an integer x, and log2 x and log10 x only at x = 2^k and 10^k, with -1074 <= k <= 1023 and 0 <= k <= 22 (`f64_pow2` and `f64_pow10`); and sin x, cos x, tan x, atan x, asin x, acos x and atan2(y, x) only at x = ±0, x = 1 for acos and y = ±0 with x > 0 for atan2, as each of them, where it is an `f64`, makes cos s rational for a rational s that is 0 only there: s = x for cos x, s = 2x for sin x and tan x, as cos 2x is 1 - 2 (sin x)^2 and (1 - (tan x)^2)/(1 + (tan x)^2), s = 2r for the results r of atan x, asin x and atan2(y, x), as cos 2r is (1 - x^2)/(1 + x^2), 1 - 2 x^2 and (x^2 - y^2)/(x^2 + y^2) (`Ratan2_polar` of `AtanReal.v`), and s = r for the result r of acos x, as cos r = x. At those arguments, the rules `near_one`, `away` and `toward_zero` of `x = ±0`, the rules of `exp10` and `log10` for `POW10` and that of `atan2` for `y = ±0` and `x > 0` return the result, and the evaluations of the other cases give the exact point at every level: `ev_exp2_exact` in [`DyExpPoint.v`](theories/DyExpPoint.v) the point 2^m at an integer m other than 0, as (m - x) ln 2 = 0 there, `ev_log2_exact` in [`DyLogPoint.v`](theories/DyLogPoint.v) the point k at 2^k, from `reduce_pow2` and `atanh_series_zero` in [`DyLogSeries.v`](theories/DyLogSeries.v), which show that `reduce` gives the exponent k and the point 0 at 2^k and `atanh_series` the point 0 at the point 0, `ev_ln_one` and `ev_acosh_one` there the point 0 at 1, and `ev_acos_one` in `DyAtanExact.v` the point 0 at 1, by computation at each of the 64 levels. The others trace the evaluations operation by operation with the lemmas of [`DyExactPoint.v`](theories/DyExactPoint.v), which take the existence of each result from those of `DyDriver.v`, so that these evaluations do not panic there. `driver_exact` of `DyPown.v` gives the nonzero points to the driver, and `driver_exact_zero` of `DyExactDriver.v` the point 0, at which `to_f64` gives `zero` in both directions, so that `ziv_at` passes at every level (`ziv_at_exact_zero`). `exp2` tries its fast path before the driver, and where that returns at such an argument, it returns the result too (`fast_exp2_exact` in `DyExpExact.v`, from `fast_exp2_dir_correct`). So do `ln`, `log2`, `log10` and `acosh`: their fast paths rounded in a direction return `None` at 1 (`fast_ln_one` and `fast_log2_one` in `FastLog.v`, `fast_acosh_one` in `FastAhyp.v`, and by computation for `log10`), and where that of `log2` returns at 2^k other than 1, it returns k, and so does that of `log10` at 10^k, which it tries before the rule for `POW10` (`fast_log2_exact` and `fast_log10_exact` in `DyLogExact.v`, from `fast_log2_dir_correct` and `fast_log10_dir_correct`). `<f>_dir_exact` is about the Rust model, with `Dyadic.ziv`. It comes from `<f>_dir_exact_with`, which holds for every driver `z` for the 14 functions whose rules settle all these arguments, and for every `z` with `driver_exact z` for `exp2`, with `driver_exact_zero z` for `ln`, `acosh` and `acos`, and with both for `log2`, which `ziv_strict` also has (`ziv_strict_exact` and `ziv_strict_exact_zero`). The others have the same form as the ones below, with the domain and the zero named above:

```coq
Definition exp_not_quadratic : Prop :=
  forall p q : Z, (0 < q)%Z -> p <> 0%Z ->
  forall a b c : Z, (a <> 0 \/ b <> 0 \/ c <> 0)%Z ->
  IZR a * exp (IZR p / IZR q) ^ 2 + IZR b * exp (IZR p / IZR q) + IZR c <> 0.

Theorem exp_irrational :
  forall p q a b : Z, (0 < q)%Z -> p <> 0%Z -> (0 < b)%Z ->
  exp (IZR p / IZR q) <> IZR a / IZR b.

Theorem cos_irrational :
  forall p q a b : Z, (0 < q)%Z -> p <> 0%Z -> (0 < b)%Z ->
  cos (IZR p / IZR q) <> IZR a / IZR b.

Theorem pow2_rational :
  forall p q a b : Z, (0 < q)%Z -> (0 < b)%Z ->
  Rpower 2 (IZR p / IZR q) = IZR a / IZR b -> (q | p)%Z.

Definition driver_exact_zero (z : (nat -> option Dyadic.encl) -> bool -> nat -> option f64) : Prop :=
  forall ev (up : bool) start,
  (start <= 64)%nat ->
  (forall n, level_ok n -> exists a, ev n = Some a /\ encl_ok n a /\
     lo_R a = 0 /\ hi_R a = 0) ->
  z ev up start = Some zero.

Theorem exp2_dir_exact_with :
  forall z, driver_exact z ->
  forall x (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite v = true -> Rexp2 (B2R x) = B2R v ->
  DyadicExp.exp2_dir_with z x up = Some v.

Theorem sinh_dir_exact :
  exp_not_quadratic ->
  forall x (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite v = true -> sinh (B2R x) = B2R v ->
  (B2R v <> 0 \/ v = x) ->
  DyadicExp.sinh_dir x up = Some v.

Theorem ln_dir_exact :
  forall x (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> log_dom (B2R x) -> is_finite v = true -> ln (B2R x) = B2R v ->
  (B2R v <> 0 \/ v = zero) ->
  DyadicLog.ln_dir x up = Some v.

Theorem atan2_dir_exact :
  forall y x (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite y = true -> is_finite x = true -> atan2_dom (B2R y) (B2R x) ->
  is_finite v = true -> Ratan2 (B2R y) (B2R x) = B2R v ->
  (B2R v <> 0 \/ v = y) ->
  DyadicAtan.atan2_dir y x up = Some v.

Theorem cos_dir_exact :
  forall x (v : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite v = true -> cos (B2R x) = B2R v ->
  DyadicTrig.cos_dir x up = Some v.
```

`k_pow_c` and `k_pown_c` in `DyPoint.v` show that `k_pow` and `k_pown`, the kernel's two functions as fields of a record `point`, satisfy `pow_c` and `pown_c` of `Contract.v`, with `kernel_field2_correct` for `pow`, as for `atan2` but with `x` as the first argument, and `kernel_field_z_correct`, its counterpart for a function of an `f64` and an integer, in a field made with `kernel_field_z`, for `pown`, so that `kernel_pow_correct` and `kernel_pown_correct` give `pow_correct` and `powi_correct` for a record `point` whose fields are these. At the special values of `pow_c` and `pown_c`, the proofs use the six lemmas of `pow` above and `pown_dir_inf` and `pown_dir_zero_neg`, with `k_pow_const` and `k_pown_const`, which show that the field returns `v` at both `RNDD` and `RNDU` where both directions return the same `f64` `v`:

```coq
Theorem k_pow_c : pow_c k_pow.

Theorem k_pown_c : pown_c k_pown.

Theorem kernel_pow_correct :
  forall first_min first_max x y, valid x -> valid y ->
  is_tightest (image2_set pow_dom Rpow x y) (Interval.pow kernel_point first_min first_max x y).

Theorem kernel_pown_correct :
  forall n x, is_i32 n -> valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.powi kernel_point n x = Some y /\
    is_tightest (image_dom (pown_dom n) (fun r => powerRZ r n) x) y.
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/pow/tests.rs` take `pow` and `pown`, in both directions, at the special values crossed with each other and with ordinary values (`special_values`), at pairs of special and simple values, at which they assert that `4^1.5` rounded down is 8 and `9^0.5` rounded up is 3 (`special_and_exact`), at fixed arguments of the tests of MPFR 4.2.2 (`tests/tpow.c`) and of pxfm 0.1.30 (`src/pow.rs`), as `x^y` but at `x < 0` and, where `y` is an `i32`, as `x^n`, with the roundings that those tests give at 53 bits where they give them, among them the three worst cases of tpow.c's `check_pow_ui`, `x^35` and two `x^36`, of which the `x^36` need about 110 bits, and at the arguments of tpow.c's `coverage`, `0.75^99.5`, `0.75^100.5` and `0.75^101.5` with the arguments next to them, and `(-2)^(2^i + 1)` for `i` from 8 to 30 (`fixed_cases`), at 2,000 random arguments, `x` log-uniform over (0, MAX], `y` uniform in [-40, 40] and `n` in [-40, 40], with `x^n` at `x` and `-x` (`random`), at 220,000 more, 100,000 with `y` of any sign and exponent, 20,000 with `|y|` from 2^-60 to 2^13 and 100,000 with `n` uniform over `i32` (`random_wide`), and at the arguments of `regressions()`, where each mismatch that a test finds goes (`regressions_hold`). Those in `src/dyadic/pow/tests/exact.rs` take the exact cases, where both directions have to give the exact result, and arguments next to them, most of whose results are not `f64`s, where the two directions have to differ but at the `x` one ulp away in `powers_of_odd_numbers` and at the subnormal `x` one ulp away in `powers_of_two`, which only the checks common to these tests take: `x^y` at `x = 2^e` for each `e` from -1074 to 1023 other than 0, at the `y = j / 2^k` with `k` up to 10 at which `e y` is an integer in the range of the `f64`s or just out of it, and at `y` one bit finer, with, at every seventh case, the `x` and `y` one ulp away (`powers_of_two`); `x^y` at `x = r^(2^k) 2^(2^k e)` for `k` up to 5, each odd `r` from 3 to 99 with `r^(2^k) < 2^53` and the greatest such odd `r`, at `y = m / 2^k` for `m` up to 33, odd if `k > 0`, and at `e = 0`, at the ends of the range of `x` and at and next to those of `x^y`, with `x^-y` and the `x` and `y` one ulp away (`powers_of_odd_numbers`); and `x^n` and `(-x)^n` at `x = 2^e` for each `e` from -1074 to 1023, with `n` at and next to the ends of the range, ±1, ±2 and the ends of `i32`, and at `x = r 2^e` for those odd `r` and, for each `n` from 2 to 33, the greatest odd `r` with `r^n < 2^53` and the odd number after it, with `n` from 1 to the first at which `r^n >= 2^53`, and with `x^-n` (`pown_exact`). Those in `rules.rs` beside it take each rule's threshold and the arguments next to it, at the ends of the binades of `x` at which `ln_expo x` changes and at `x = 1 ± 2^-j` for some `j` and the `f64`s next to them, results at and next to 2^1024, 2^-1022 and 2^-1074 and in the subnormals, and `y` next to 1 at the ends of the range (`rules_at_thresholds`), the eight `f64`s on each side of 1, with `y` at which `y ln x` is next to the thresholds of overflow and underflow, to ±1 and to 0, with `y = ±2^j` for `j` from 52 to 1023, and with `n` at ±2^20, ±2^30 and the ends of `i32` (`near_one`), `x^2` and `x^3`, also as `x^y`, at `x` near a power of 2, where the binomial expansion gives their roundings (`known_roundings`), `x^y` at the integers `y` at and next to `±2^j` for `j` from 0 to 31, among them the ends of the range that `pow` passes to `pown_pos_ziv` and the integers next to them, with `x` the four `f64`s on each side of 1, 1/2, 2, 3 and 1.5, and `2^(q / y)` for `q` below -1074 and at it, at -1022, below 1024 and between (`integer_route`), and `3^34`, the midpoint of two `f64`s, whose roundings they assert, and which `ziv_at` settles as `x^n` at one limb in both directions (`three_to_the_34`); and those in `near_floats.rs` take `x` one ulp from a power of 2, with `y = ±n` for `n` up to 40 and next to 2^j for `j` from 6 to 52, also as `x^n` where `n` is an `i32` (`next_to_powers_of_two`), and `x^(1/2)` and `x^(-1/2)` at the squares of the `f64`s `m 2^j` for 22 odd `m` with `m^2 < 2^53`, among them the greatest such `m`, each in 7 binades, and at the `f64`s next to them (`square_roots`). They check that the two directions give the same `f64` or two adjacent ones, or NaN both, that they give, at the special values, those of a table, signs of zeros included, which are the results that MPFR gives and that `pow_c` and `pown_c` in `Contract.v` allow, and NaN for `x^y` at `x < 0` and `y != 0`, which is `pow`'s own choice, in `exact.rs` the exact result where it is an `f64` and two different `f64`s where it is not, but at the `x` one ulp away that `exact.rs` leaves to the other checks, in `integer_route`, for `x^y`, the bits that `pown` gives at `n = y` wherever `y` is an integer from -2^31 to 2^31 - 1, and the roundings that the tests of MPFR and pxfm and bounds on the results determine, where these do, and, on 64-bit Linux and macOS, that they give those of `pow_rd`, `pow_ru`, `pown_rd` and `pown_ru` of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which call MPFR's `mpfr_pow` and `mpfr_pow_si` at 53 bits, bit for bit, signs of zeros included, at each of these arguments but those of `x^y` with `x < 0`. Those in `src/dyadic/pow/tests/levels.rs` force each level from 1 to 64 limbs at 14 arguments of `x^y` and 10 of `x^n`, at some of which `|y ln x|` or `|n ln x|` is between 700 and 745, and at some next to 1 with a huge `y` or `n`, as for the exponentials above, and check that each level from 2 limbs on gives the rounding that `pow` or `pown` gives, and, on 64-bit Linux and macOS, that each enclosure holds the value that MPFR computes at 8,192 bits and that each doubling of the precision at least halves its width (`forced_levels`), and take, with the checks above, 11 arguments of `x^y` and 9 of `x^n`, the latter at `x` and `-x`, the hardest few of each set, by the distance from the result to the nearest `f64` relative to the result, that a level histogram of 4,096 random arguments per set and a search of 10^7 per set found: `x^y` one ulp from an exact case, with `|y ln x|` from 100 to 745, next to 1 and of moderate size, and `x^n` with `n` = -17, -5, -3, -2, 2^20 and `i32::MAX` and with `n` of a bit length uniform from 1 to 31 (`hard_cases`). Neither found an argument that needs more than 2 limbs.

[`ContractSide.v`](theories/ContractSide.v) states, for each field of a record `point` other than those of `cbrt` and `hypot`, a contract weaker than that of `Contract.v`, which the functions of the kernel satisfy at the cap of `ziv` too: `on_side rnd r v` says that `v` is at or below `r` for `RNDD` and at or above it for `RNDU`, not a NaN, and an infinity only of that side, and `point_side` that a field is on the side `rnd` of the function at the finite arguments in the domain. Each `<f>_s`, such as `exp_s`, is `<f>_c` of `Contract.v` with `point_side` in place of `point_correct` and the special values unchanged, and `sin_s` and `cos_s` also ask for the signs that `quadrant` reads (see "What is assumed"). `exp_encloses_side` and the other `<f>_encloses_side` in [`SideTheorems.v`](theories/SideTheorems.v) show that the 21 interval functions return, for a record whose fields satisfy these contracts, intervals that contain the image of their argument (`encloses` of `Ieee1788.v`), though not always the tightest ones. The proof goes through `side_tight p` of [`SideTight.v`](theories/SideTight.v), the record whose field of each function `f` is `f` rounded once at the finite arguments in its domain and `p`'s field elsewhere. Its fields satisfy `Contract.v` (`exp_tight_c` and the others), so that `exp_correct_point` and the others give the tightest intervals for it. `p`'s fields are at or beyond its fields in the direction of each rounding (`exp_tight_out` and the others), so that `p` gives intervals that contain those (`exp_wider` and the others in [`SideMono.v`](theories/SideMono.v) and [`SideMonoB.v`](theories/SideMonoB.v)), and the two records give the same `quadrant` at every argument (`quadrant_tight`), by the signs of `sin_s` and `cos_s`, so that `Interval::sin`, `Interval::cos` and `Interval::tan` take the same branches for both. There is no such contract for the fields of `cbrt` and `hypot`, as the kernel computes them without `ziv`, and returns at every argument, the correct roundings at the finite ones (`k_cbrt_some`, `k_hypot_some`, `k_cbrt_c` and `k_hypot_c` below): `side_tight p` keeps `p`'s fields of these two, and `kernel_point_rust` has those of `kernel_point`. `kernel_point_rust` in [`DyPointSide.v`](theories/DyPointSide.v) is the record of the functions of the kernel as the Rust computes them, with `Dyadic.ziv` and its cap, where they return, and the result rounded by definition only where the Rust panics. `k_exp_rust_s` and the others show that its fields satisfy these contracts, from `exp_dir_side` and the others, and for sin and cos also from `sin_dir_sign` and `cos_dir_sign`. So `kernel_exp_encloses` and the others show that the interval functions with the kernel return intervals that contain the image of their argument, also where a call of the kernel reaches the cap of `ziv` without a test passing, at every argument at which no call of the kernel panics:

```coq
Definition on_side (rnd : mpfr_rnd) (r : R) (v : f64) : Prop :=
  match v with
  | B754_infinity s => s = match rnd with RNDD => true | RNDU => false end
  | B754_nan => False
  | _ => match rnd with RNDD => B2R v <= r | RNDU => r <= B2R v end
  end.

Definition sin_s (h : mpfr_rnd -> f64 -> f64) : Prop :=
  point_side real_dom sin h /\
  forall x : f64, is_finite x = true ->
  (Model.lt (h RNDD x) Model.zero = true <-> sin (B2R x) < 0).

Theorem sin_encloses_side :
  forall p, sin_s (p_sin p) -> cos_s (p_cos p) ->
  forall has_fma first x, valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.sin p has_fma first x = Some y /\ encloses (image_dom real_dom sin x) y.

Theorem kernel_exp_encloses :
  forall x, valid x -> encloses (image_dom real_dom exp x) (Interval.exp kernel_point_rust x).
```

The same functions rounded to nearest, ties to even, `exp_near(x)` and the others of `src/dyadic/*.rs`, which the `_rn` functions of `inari::cr` in `src/cr.rs` call, have theorems of the same form, with `near_round` of [`NearF64.v`](theories/NearF64.v) (see "What is assumed") in place of `mpfr_round`. `to_f64_near_correct` in [`DyConvNear.v`](theories/DyConvNear.v) shows that `to_f64_near` rounds a dyadic number to nearest as `near_round` says, and gives +0 for a zero, from the lemmas on rounding a window of bits to nearest of [`NearRound.v`](theories/NearRound.v). `Dyadic.ziv_near ev start`, the model of `ziv_near`, tries the levels as `ziv` does, until the two bounds of the enclosure, rounded to nearest, have the same bits, which, unlike `==`, tell -0 from +0, and where none passes, `ziv_near_cap`, which evaluates 64 limbs and returns that rounding if the test passes there, and otherwise the rounded lower bound if its last bit is 0 and the rounded upper bound if not. `ziv_near_strict ev start` of [`DyDriverNear.v`](theories/DyDriverNear.v) tries the levels 1, 2, 4, ..., 64 in the same way, with `None` where none passes or an evaluation panics. Where it returns, it returns the rounding to nearest (`ziv_near_strict_correct`), and `ziv_near` returns the same value (`ziv_near_strict_ziv`). At the cap, where no test passes, `ziv_near` returns the rounding to nearest of a bound of the enclosure at 64 limbs (`ziv_near_cap_round`), which is not shown to be that of the number it encloses:

```coq
Definition near_round (r : R) (v : f64) : Prop :=
  if Rle_dec (bpow radix2 1024 - bpow radix2 970) (Rabs r) then v = B754_infinity (Rlt_bool r 0)
  else
    is_finite v = true /\
    B2R v = round radix2 (FLT_exp (-1074) 53) ZnearestE r /\
    (r <> 0 -> B2R v = 0 -> Bsign v = Rlt_bool r 0).

Definition near_post (z : (nat -> option Dyadic.encl) -> nat -> option f64)
    (post : R -> f64 -> Prop) : Prop :=
  forall ev start r v,
  (forall n a, level_ok n -> ev n = Some a -> encl_ok n a /\ encl_has a r) ->
  z ev start = Some v -> post r v.

Theorem ziv_near_strict_correct : near_post ziv_near_strict near_round.

Theorem ziv_near_cap_round :
  forall ev v,
  (forall a, ev 64%nat = Some a -> encl_ok 64 a) ->
  Dyadic.ziv_near_cap ev = Some v ->
  exists a, ev 64%nat = Some a /\ (near_round (lo_R a) v \/ near_round (hi_R a) v).
```

`exp_near_correct` in [`DyExpNear.v`](theories/DyExpNear.v) shows that `exp_near(x)` of `src/dyadic/exp.rs` returns, at a finite `x` and wherever it returns by a rule, by the fast path (`fast_exp_near_correct` above, `fast_ln_near_correct` and the others for `ln`, `log2` and `log10`, `fast_sin_near_correct` and the others for `sin`, `cos` and `tan`, `fast_pow_near_correct` for `pow` and `pown`, and `fast_atan_near_correct` and the others for `atan`, `asin`, `acos` and `atan2`) or by a rounding test of `ziv_near` that passes, e^x rounded to nearest as `near_round` says, and `exp_near_strict` that where `exp_near_with ziv_near_strict` returns, `exp_near(x)` returns the same value. `exp2_near_correct` to `tanh_near_correct` in `DyExpNear.v`, `ln_near_correct` to `atanh_near_correct` in [`DyLogNear.v`](theories/DyLogNear.v), `atan_near_correct`, `asin_near_correct`, `acos_near_correct` and `atan2_near_correct` in [`DyAtanNear.v`](theories/DyAtanNear.v), `sin_near_correct`, `cos_near_correct` and `tan_near_correct` in [`DyTrigNear.v`](theories/DyTrigNear.v), and `pow_near_correct` and `pown_near_correct` in [`DyPowNear.v`](theories/DyPowNear.v), each with its `_strict` theorem, state the same of the twenty other functions rounded to nearest, at the finite arguments in the domains of their directed theorems above. `DyadicExp.exp_near x` in `Model.v` is `exp_near(x)`, with `None` where the Rust panics, and `DyadicExp.exp_near_with z x` is the same with the driver `z` in place of `Dyadic.ziv_near`. The loop rests on the enclosures of the directed proofs (`ev_exp_correct` and the others) and `ziv_near_strict_correct`. The rules are those of the directed functions, with the real numbers that round to nearest to the `f64`s that they return, the cells of [`NearCells.v`](theories/NearCells.v), in place of the directed roundings, such as `near_zero`, that the numbers in (0, 2^-1075] round to +0, and `near_inf`, that those from 2^1024 - 2^970 up round to +∞. Where the bound of a directed rule leaves its result half an ulp or more from 1, `x` or ±1, the rule rounded to nearest has a threshold of its own: 2^-27 (`TINY_NEAR`) in place of 2^-26 for sinh, cosh, tanh, asinh, atanh, atan, sin, cos, tan and the quotients of atan2, and 20 in place of 19 for tanh. sin, cos and tan rounded to nearest have no counterparts of the rules `sin_small`, `cos_small` and `tan_small` of the directed functions. Rounded to nearest, Ziv's strategy cannot settle an argument whose result is the midpoint of two adjacent `f64`s unless the midpoint is a bound of an enclosure, as in an exact one, since the two bounds of an enclosure that holds it strictly inside round to different `f64`s. `exp10_near` has a rule at 23, where 10^23 = 5^23 2^23 is such a midpoint, which returns the even neighbour, `1e23`, and 2^-1075, which `exp2_near` gives at -1075, has an exact enclosure. `atan2_near`, for `x > 0`, first calls `midpoint_quotient`, which returns the `f64` next to `y / x` toward 0 where `y / x` is an odd multiple of 2^-1075 below 2^-1021 in magnitude, the midpoint of two `f64`s, which atan(y / x) is just inside of. `midpoint_quotient_correct` in [`DyAtanMidQ.v`](theories/DyAtanMidQ.v) shows that this `f64` is atan(y / x) rounded to nearest. For `x < 2^996`, `midpoint_quotient` decides the quotient in `f64`: `exact_div` at `2y` returns only `2y / x` exactly (`exact_div_correct`), below 2^-1021 an odd last bit of that `f64` makes `y / x` an odd multiple of 2^-1075, and the `f64` with that bit cleared, halved exactly, is the `f64` next to `y / x` toward 0. For `x >= 2^996` (`midpoint_quotient_dyadic_correct`), it follows from `div_mid` in [`MidQuot.v`](theories/MidQuot.v), that the quotient that it computes is `y / x` exactly, and `to_f64_mid` in [`MidRound.v`](theories/MidRound.v), that `to_f64` rounds it toward 0. On both paths, [`NearAtanMid.v`](theories/NearAtanMid.v) gives that atan rounds to nearest there to that `f64`. `pow_near` calls, after the fast path, `exact_near` in place of `exact`, which is `exact` with the bound 2^54 in place of 2^53 on `q = r^p` (`small_power_near`), and returns x^y rounded to nearest where x^y is `q 2^e` with `e >= -1075` below 2^1024. `exact_near_sound` in [`DyPowNearExact.v`](theories/DyPowNearExact.v) shows that an `f64` that `exact_near` returns is x^y rounded to nearest, and `exact_near_complete` that `exact_near` returns at every such argument with `x ≠ 1` and `y ≠ 0`, which `pow_near` settles before it calls `exact_near`, the `f64`s and the midpoints of two adjacent `f64`s among them, both from `DyPowArith.v` and `DyPowExact.v`. `pow_near_midpoint` gives from it, as the fast path before `exact_near` returns or passes on and `pow_near_correct` gives the rounding to nearest wherever `pow_near` returns, that `pow_near`, the Rust, returns x^y rounded to nearest at every finite `x > 0` and finite `y` at which x^y is such a number:

```coq
Theorem exp_near_correct :
  forall x v,
  is_finite x = true -> DyadicExp.exp_near_with ziv_near_strict x = Some v ->
  near_round (exp (B2R x)) v.

Theorem pow_near_correct :
  forall x y v,
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite y = true -> pow_dom (B2R x) (B2R y) ->
  DyadicPow.pow_near_with ziv_near_strict x y = Some v ->
  near_round (Rpow (B2R x) (B2R y)) v.

Theorem exact_near_complete :
  forall x y q e,
  is_finite x = true -> (0 < B2R x)%R -> B2R x <> 1%R -> is_finite y = true -> B2R y <> 0%R ->
  0 < q < 2 ^ 54 -> -1075 <= e -> e + Z.log2 q <= 1023 ->
  Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y) = (IZR q * bpow radix2 e)%R ->
  exists v, DyadicPow.exact_near x y = Some (Some v).

Theorem pow_near_midpoint :
  forall x y q e,
  is_finite x = true -> (0 < B2R x)%R -> is_finite y = true ->
  0 < q < 2 ^ 54 -> -1075 <= e -> e + Z.log2 q <= 1023 ->
  Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y) = (IZR q * bpow radix2 e)%R ->
  exists v, DyadicPow.pow_near x y = Some v /\ near_round (Rpower (B2R x) (B2R y)) v.
```

[`CrPow.v`](theories/CrPow.v) is about `pow_dir` and `pow_near_signed` of `src/cr.rs`, with which `pow_rd`, `pow_ru` and `pow_rn` of `inari::cr` give MPFR's values at a finite `x < 0` and at `x = -∞`, which are `Cr.pow_dir` and `Cr.pow_near_signed` in `Model.v`. `cr_pow_dir_neg` and `cr_pow_near_neg` show that they return, at a finite `x < 0` and a finite integer `y = k`, `x^k` rounded in the direction asked and to nearest, where the kernel's function that they call returns by a rule or a passing rounding test, `cr_pow_dir_dom` and `cr_pow_near_dom` the same of x^y in `pow_dom`, and `cr_pow_dir_strict` and `cr_pow_near_strict` that the Rust returns the same value there. The other functions of `inari::cr` but `sqrt_rd`, `sqrt_ru` and `sqrt_rn` (see below) call one function of the kernel each and nothing else, so its theorems are theirs:

```coq
Theorem cr_pow_near_neg :
  forall x y k v,
  is_finite x = true -> (B2R x < 0)%R -> is_finite y = true -> B2R y = IZR k ->
  Cr.pow_near_signed_with ziv_near_strict x y = Some v ->
  near_round (powerRZ (B2R x) k) v.
```

The cube root and `hypot` of `src/dyadic/root.rs`, which `cbrt_rd`, `cbrt_ru`, `cbrt_rn`, `hypot_rd`, `hypot_ru` and `hypot_rn` of `inari::cr` call, first try a fast path in `f64` (below), and behind it do not use Ziv's strategy, so their theorems hold at every finite argument, with no condition on rules or rounding tests. Behind the fast path, each computes, exactly in integers, the floor `s` of its result scaled by a power of two, with `s >= 2^53`, and whether the scaled result is above `s`, and rounds the result to odd at the bits of `s` and one more. `odd_correct` in [`RootRound.v`](theories/RootRound.v) shows that `odd` returns that number, and `odd_window_dir` and `odd_window_near` that it rounds toward -∞, toward +∞ and to nearest as the result does, the latter from Flocq's `round_N_odd` (Boldo and Melquiond, 2008, Theorem 2). `icbrt_correct` in [`DyIcbrt.v`](theories/DyIcbrt.v) shows that `icbrt`, Newton's iteration on integers from a seed that `f64` arithmetic computes, returns floor(cbrt X) and whether X is a cube, and `hypot_odd_correct` in [`DyHypotOdd.v`](theories/DyHypotOdd.v) that `hypot_odd` returns `s` and that bit for hypot, from `hypot_cut` in [`RootReal.v`](theories/RootReal.v) where the binades of `|x|` and `|y|` are 27 or more apart, and otherwise from `hypot_exact_branch` in [`DyHypotExact.v`](theories/DyHypotExact.v), the integer square root of the exact sum of the squares by `isqrt_correct`. `cbrt_dir_correct` and `cbrt_near_correct` in [`DyCbrt.v`](theories/DyCbrt.v) and `hypot_dir_correct` and `hypot_near_correct` in [`DyHypot.v`](theories/DyHypot.v) give the results, from the theorems of the fast paths below where those return and from these elsewhere, and `cbrt_special`, `hypot_inf`, `hypot_nan` and `hypot_zeros` the values at the zeros, the infinities and NaN: `x` itself for cbrt, and for hypot +∞ if `x` or `y` is infinite, NaN otherwise if one is NaN, and +0 at two zeros. `Rcbrt` is the real cube root of `Elementary.v` (see "What is assumed"). [`CrRoot.v`](theories/CrRoot.v) is about `sqrt_rd`, `sqrt_ru` and `sqrt_rn` of `inari::cr`, which call the backend's `sqrt1_rd` and `sqrt1_ru` and `f64::sqrt`: `cr_sqrt_dir_correct` restates `sqrt1_rd_correct` and `sqrt1_ru_correct` of `SqrtRdRu.v` with `mpfr_round`, with or without an FMA, `cr_sqrt_rn_correct` gives `near_round` from Flocq's `Bsqrt_correct`, as `f64::sqrt` is `Bsqrt` (see "What is assumed"), and `cr_sqrt_zero`, `cr_sqrt_inf` and `cr_sqrt_nan` give a zero at the same zero, +∞ at +∞ and NaN at NaN, at -∞ and at a finite `x < 0`:

```coq
Theorem cbrt_dir_correct :
  forall x up,
  is_finite x = true ->
  exists v, DyadicRoot.cbrt x up = Some v /\
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (Rcbrt (B2R x)) (with_val v).

Theorem hypot_near_correct :
  forall x y,
  is_finite x = true -> is_finite y = true ->
  exists v, DyadicRoot.hypot_near x y = Some v /\
  near_round (sqrt (B2R x * B2R x + B2R y * B2R y)) v.

Theorem cr_sqrt_dir_correct :
  forall has_fma (x : f64) (up : bool),
  is_finite x = true -> 0 <= B2R x ->
  exists v : f64,
  (if up then Generic.sqrt1_ru has_fma x else Generic.sqrt1_rd has_fma x) = Some v /\
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (sqrt (B2R x)) (with_val v).
```

The fast paths, after the zeros, the infinities and NaN and before `cbrt_odd` and `hypot_odd`, are `fast_cbrt_dir`, `fast_cbrt_near`, `fast_hypot` and `fast_hypot_near` in `src/dyadic/root.rs`. Each, but at the gap rule of `hypot` below, computes its result scaled by `2^-e` as a double-double `h + l` with `h = RN(h + l)` and decides its rounding with `round_dir_err` or `round_near_err` and its bound, by `round_dir_err_correct` and `round_near_err_correct` of `FastRoundErr.v`; where they return `None`, the kernel computes the result. `fast_cbrt` follows CORE-MATH's `cbrt`. `cbrt_split_bits` and `cbrt_zz_rr` in [`FastCbrtReduce.v`](theories/FastCbrtReduce.v) show that it writes `|x|` from its bits, after the leading zeros of a subnormal, as `zz 2^(3 e)` with `zz` in [1, 8), and that `rr` is within `2^-53 / zz` of `1 / zz`. Its approximation `y` of `cbrt zz`, from a cubic, a step of a third-order iteration and a factor `2^(i/3)`, has no proved bound: `cbrt_step` checks that the residual `y^3 - zz`, as computed, is at most 2^-32 in magnitude, and `cbrt_step_correct` in [`FastCbrtStep.v`](theories/FastCbrtStep.v) shows that its Newton step is then within 2^-65 of `cbrt zz`, from `cbrt_newton`, that the step on real numbers is within 2^-66 of it, and `cbrt_step_err`, that its roundings move it by at most 2^-82, in [`FastCbrtNewton.v`](theories/FastCbrtNewton.v). `fast_cbrt_correct` in [`FastCbrt.v`](theories/FastCbrt.v) puts them together, and `fast_cbrt_dir_correct` and `fast_cbrt_near_correct` give cbrt x rounded, with the sign put back by `Rcbrt_opp`, `pown_rnd_opp` and `near_opp`. `fast_hypot` and `fast_hypot_near` return `big = max(|x|, |y|)`, or the `f64` after it rounded up, where the biased exponents of `big` and `small = min(|x|, |y|)` differ by 27 or more, which `hypot_far_dir` and `hypot_far_near` in [`FastHypotFar.v`](theories/FastHypotFar.v) prove from the bits, as `hypot(big, small)` is then above `big` by less than half an ulp of `big`. Elsewhere `hypot_dd` scales both by the same power of two, sums their squares in double-double and takes the square root with `dd_sqrt`: `hypot_sum_sq_correct` in [`FastHypotSum.v`](theories/FastHypotSum.v) shows that the sum is within 2^-102 of the sum of the squares of the scaled `big` and `small`, and `hypot_dd_correct` in [`FastHypotDd.v`](theories/FastHypotDd.v), with `dd_sqrt_correct`, that the root is within 2^-98 of `hypot(big, small) 2^-e`. `fast_hypot_dir_correct` and `fast_hypot_near_correct` in [`FastHypot.v`](theories/FastHypot.v) give hypot(x, y) rounded. `fast_cbrt_dir_fma`, `fast_cbrt_near_fma`, `fast_hypot_dir_fma` and `fast_hypot_near_fma` show that an FMA changes neither fast path, from `cbrt_step_fma`, `hypot_dd_fma`, `hypot_sum_sq_fma` and `dd_sqrt_fma`, so `DyadicRoot.cbrt` and the others pass `false`, as for e^x:

```coq
Theorem fast_cbrt_correct :
  forall fma x h l e,
  is_finite x = true -> B2R x <> 0 ->
  DyadicRoot.fast_cbrt fma x = Some (h, l, e) ->
  is_finite h = true /\ is_finite l = true /\ B2R h = RN (B2R h + B2R l) /\
  (-358 <= e <= 341)%Z /\
  Rabs (Rcbrt (Rabs (B2R x)) * bpow radix2 (- e) - (B2R h + B2R l)) <= bpow radix2 (-65).

Theorem hypot_dd_correct :
  forall fma big small h l e,
  is_finite big = true -> is_finite small = true ->
  0 < B2R small <= B2R big ->
  (Z.shiftr (to_bits big) 52 - Z.shiftr (to_bits small) 52 < 27)%Z ->
  DyadicRoot.hypot_dd fma big small = Some (h, l, e) ->
  is_finite h = true /\ is_finite l = true /\ B2R h = RN (B2R h + B2R l) /\
  (-1021 <= e <= 1022)%Z /\
  Rabs (sqrt (B2R big * B2R big + B2R small * B2R small) * bpow radix2 (- e)
        - (B2R h + B2R l)) <= bpow radix2 (-98).
```

`k_cbrt` and `k_hypot` in `DyPoint.v` are the kernel's cube root and `hypot` as fields of a record `point`, `kernel_field` of `DyadicRoot.cbrt` and `kernel_field2` of `DyadicRoot.hypot`. `k_cbrt_some` and `k_hypot_some` show that the Rust returns them at every argument, from the theorems above, and `k_cbrt_c` and `k_hypot_c` that they satisfy `cbrt_c` and `hypot_c` of `Contract.v`, so that `kernel_cbrt_correct` and `kernel_hypot_correct`, and `kernel_dec_cbrt_correct` and `kernel_dec_hypot_correct` in `DyPointDec.v`, state the theorems of `Cbrt.v` and `Hypot.v` for `kernel_point`, with no condition on the calls of the kernel. `kernel_cbrt_encloses` and `kernel_hypot_encloses` in `DyPointSide.v` follow from them, as `kernel_point_rust` has the same fields of `cbrt` and `hypot`:

```coq
Theorem k_cbrt_some : forall rnd x, DyadicRoot.cbrt x (up_of rnd) = Some (k_cbrt rnd x).

Theorem kernel_hypot_correct :
  forall x y, valid x -> valid y ->
  exists z, Interval.hypot kernel_point x y = Some z /\ is_tightest (image2 Rhypot x y) z.
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/root/tests.rs` check cbrt and hypot in both directions and to nearest against an exact oracle of their own, which compares the cubes or squares of the results and of their midpoints with those of the root in integers, with arithmetic of its own: at the special values and every pair of them, at random arguments, log-uniform with a random sign and uniform in (-1, 1), at pairs with a gap of 0 to 60 binades, in every binade of cbrt, at the exact cubes and the `f64`s next to them, at scaled Pythagorean triples, whose hypotenuses are `f64`s or midpoints of two adjacent `f64`s, at gaps of 26, 27 and 28 binades and the `f64`s next to them, at results that cross the thresholds of overflow, and at subnormal arguments, and at the arguments of `regressions()`. That oracle checks the functions with their fast paths, and `fast_cbrt_agrees_with_kernel` and `fast_hypot_agrees_with_kernel` check that the fast paths give the `f64`s of the kernel on random sets of their own and that most of their rounding tests pass there; `fast_cbrt_declines_cubes`, `fast_cbrt_near_two`, `fast_hypot_declines_345`, `fast_hypot_declines_triples` and `hypot_dd_range` that they leave to the kernel the exact results in both directions, the midpoints of hypot to nearest, an `h` of 2 and the pairs that `hypot_dd` does not take; and `fast_hypot_gap_rule` that the rule for a gap of 27 on exponent fields fires only where the gap of `hypot_odd` is 27 or more. The tests of `src/cr.rs` compare `cbrt`, `hypot` and `sqrt` of `inari::cr` in their three roundings with MPFR at special values, random arguments, exact cubes and squares, the midpoints of `hypot`, both sides of its cut, subnormal arguments and results near MAX.

The tests of each family of `src/dyadic/` check the function rounded to nearest at the arguments at which they check its directions, that it gives one of the two directions, or NaN where they are NaN, and, on 64-bit Linux and macOS, that it gives, bit for bit, signs of zeros included, the `f64` of the `<f>_rn` of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which rounds MPFR's result to nearest once, with the subnormals of binary64 (`mpfr_near!`). `near_rules_at_their_thresholds` and `near_exact_values` in `src/dyadic/exp/tests.rs` take each threshold of the rules rounded to nearest and the `f64`s next to it, 2^x at -1074, -1075 and -1076, and 10^x at the integers from 1 to 24. `midpoint_quotients` in `src/dyadic/atan/tests.rs` takes atan2 at 2,003 quotients `y / x` that `midpoint_quotient` settles and at the `f64`s next to `y`, at which it does not. `odd_powers` and `subnormal_midpoints` in `src/dyadic/pow/tests/near.rs` take x^n, and x^y with `y = n`, `y = n / 2` and, in `odd_powers`, `y = n / 2^j` for each `j >= 2` at which the base `r^(2^j) 2^(2^j k)` is an `f64`, at midpoints `r^n 2^(k n)` for odd `r` with `r^n` in [2^53, 2^54), and at the midpoints `(2j + 1) 2^-1075` below 2^-1021, with the `f64`s next to their arguments, against the even neighbour computed in integers. The `levels.rs` tests check that `ziv_near_at` at each level from 1 to 64 limbs either fails or gives the rounding to nearest, and that `ziv_near_cap` gives it. The tests of `src/cr.rs` compare each function of `inari::cr` in its three roundings with MPFR at special arguments, at the thresholds of the kernel and the `f64`s next to them, and at seeded random arguments, `pow` at `x < 0` and `x = -∞` among them, and `src/cr/tests/mpfr/hard_cases.rs` at a sample of 1,389 of the hard-to-round arguments that CORE-MATH lists in its `src/binary64/<f>/<f>.wc` files.

`E_tightest` in [`ConstLn.v`](theories/ConstLn.v) shows that `Interval::E` and `DecInterval::E` are the tightest intervals around e, the base of natural logarithms, as the doc comment of `Interval::E` says, and that of `DecInterval::E`, which is `Interval::E` decorated `com`, and that this decoration is the strongest (`strongest0`):

```coq
Theorem E_tightest :
  is_tightest (fun r => r = exp 1) Interval.E /\
  is_tightest (fun r => r = exp 1) (itv DecInterval.E) /\
  strongest0 (dec DecInterval.E) (exp 1) (itv DecInterval.E).
```

`FRAC_1_PI_tightest`, `FRAC_1_SQRT_2_tightest`, `FRAC_2_PI_tightest`, `FRAC_2_SQRT_PI_tightest`, `FRAC_PI_2_tightest`, `FRAC_PI_3_tightest`, `FRAC_PI_4_tightest`, `FRAC_PI_6_tightest`, `FRAC_PI_8_tightest`, `LN_10_tightest`, `LN_2_tightest`, `LOG10_2_tightest`, `LOG10_E_tightest`, `LOG2_10_tightest`, `LOG2_E_tightest`, `PI_tightest`, `SQRT_2_tightest` and `TAU_tightest` state the same for the other 18 constants, with `/ PI`, `/ sqrt 2`, `2 / PI`, `2 / sqrt PI`, `PI / 2`, `PI / 3`, `PI / 4`, `PI / 6`, `PI / 8`, `ln 10`, `ln 2`, `Rlog10 2`, `Rlog10 (exp 1)`, `Rlog2 10`, `Rlog2 (exp 1)`, `PI`, `sqrt 2` and `2 * PI` in place of `exp 1`. `exp`, `ln`, `sqrt` and `PI` are Stdlib's, and `Rlog2` and `Rlog10` are the point functions of `log2` and `log10` in `Elementary.v`. [`ConstPi.v`](theories/ConstPi.v) proves the theorems for `PI`, `TAU` and `FRAC_PI_2` to `FRAC_PI_8`, [`ConstInv.v`](theories/ConstInv.v) for `FRAC_1_PI`, `FRAC_2_PI`, `FRAC_2_SQRT_PI`, `SQRT_2` and `FRAC_1_SQRT_2`, `ConstLn.v` for `E`, `LN_2` and `LN_10`, and [`ConstLog.v`](theories/ConstLog.v) for `LOG10_2`, `LOG10_E`, `LOG2_10` and `LOG2_E`. `is_tightest` of the set that holds only the value says that the constant is the tightest interval that contains the value, as for the operations above. `def_com!` makes each of these constants of `DecInterval` from the constant of `Interval` with the same name, decorated `com`, so its interval is the same, and the second part of each theorem follows from the first. `strongest0 d c z`, defined in `Ieee1788.v` (below), says that `d` is the strongest decoration of the constant function `c()` whose computed interval is `z`. `com_strongest0` in [`ConstTight.v`](theories/ConstTight.v) shows that `com` is the strongest for any `c` if `z` is bounded, and `const_strongest0` that it is if `z` is common, which `vm_compute` checks for the interval of each constant, so the third part follows.

Each of these constants of `Interval` is `const_interval!(a, b)` for two finite floats `a` and `b`, where `b` is the float after `a`, Flocq's `Bsucc a`, which `vm_compute` checks from their bit patterns. If the value `c` lies strictly between them, `b` is the least float at or above `c`, and `-a` the least at or above `-c` (`pt_up` and `pt_dn` in `PiTight.v`), so `[a, b]` is the tightest interval that contains `c` (`const_tightest` in [`ConstTight.v`](theories/ConstTight.v), by `tightest_lanes` in `AddSub.v`). `B2Q` gives the value of a float as a rational, so that `const_tightest_Q` reduces `a < c < b` to `a < l` and `u < b` for rationals `l <= c <= u`, which `vm_compute` decides in Stdlib's rationals `Q`. The rational bounds of the values come from:

- π: `PI_Q` in `PiBounds.v` bounds π by two rationals `10^-18` apart, from the partial sums of the series of the Machin-like formula `π/4 = 2 atan(1/3) + atan(1/7)`, and `PI_bounds` is proved from it (above). These rationals multiplied by 2, or divided by 3, 4, 6 or 8, bound the values of `TAU` and `FRAC_PI_3` to `FRAC_PI_8`, and 1 and 2 divided by them those of `FRAC_1_PI` and `FRAC_2_PI`. `PI` and `FRAC_PI_2` do not go through `const_tightest_Q`: `const_tightest` applies to them directly, with `a < c < b` from `PI_bounds` (and `pb_frac_lo` and `pb_frac_hi` for π/2), and the finiteness of `a` and `b` and `Bsucc a = b` from `pt_PI_facts` and `pt_FRAC_PI_2_facts` in `PiTight.v`, which `PI_up`, `PI_dn`, `FRAC_PI_2_up` and `FRAC_PI_2_dn` use too.
- The square roots: `const_tightest_sqrt` in `ConstInv.v` compares the squares of the bounds, which are rationals, instead: `a < √2 < b` if `a² < 2 < b²`, `a < 1/√2 < b` if `2a² < 1 < 2b²`, and `a < 2/√π < b` if `a² π_hi < 4 < b² π_lo`, for positive `a` and `b` and the rational bounds `π_lo` and `π_hi` of π.
- e: `exp_Q_bounds` in [`ExpSeries.v`](theories/ExpSeries.v) bounds `exp q` for a rational `0 <= q <= 1` by the reciprocals of two partial sums of the alternating series of `exp(-q)`, which bound it below and above (Stdlib's `alternated_series_ineq`). `es_sumQ` computes these sums in `Q` with the common denominator `d^n n!` of a rational `q = p / d`, so that `vm_compute` adds them without reducing fractions.
- ln 2 and ln 10: `q < ln y` if `exp q < y`, and `ln y < q` if `0 < y < exp q`, so these bounds of `exp` bound `ln 2` by two rationals `10^-24` apart (`LN_2_Q`). The bounds of `ln 10` are above 1, where `exp_Q_bounds` does not apply, so `LN_10_Q` bounds `exp` at a third of each and cubes it, as `exp(3q) = exp(q)³`.
- The logarithms to the bases 2 and 10: `Rlog b r` is `ln r / ln b`, and `ln (exp 1) = 1`, so the values of `LOG10_2`, `LOG10_E`, `LOG2_10` and `LOG2_E` are `ln 2 / ln 10`, `1 / ln 10`, `ln 10 / ln 2` and `1 / ln 2`, which quotients of the rational bounds of `ln 2` and `ln 10` bound.

`ENTIRE_dec_correct`, `EMPTY_dec_correct` and `NAI_dec_correct` in `ConstTight.v` show that the other three constants of `DecInterval` are decorated as IEEE 1788 asks:

```coq
Theorem ENTIRE_dec_correct :
  dec DecInterval.ENTIRE = Dac /\ forall c, strongest0 Dac c (itv DecInterval.ENTIRE).

Theorem EMPTY_dec_correct : dec DecInterval.EMPTY = Trv.

Theorem NAI_dec_correct : dec DecInterval.NAI = Ill.
```

§12.12.1 of the draft P1788/D9.5 asks the decorated versions of `empty()` and `entire()` to return the empty set decorated `trv` and `[-∞, +∞]` decorated `dac`, and its §12.5.1 identifies NaI with the empty set decorated `ill`. `dac` is the strongest decoration of the constant function `c()` whose computed interval is `ENTIRE`, for every `c`, as `com` asks for a bounded computed interval.

[`Valid.v`](theories/Valid.v) shows that the hypothesis `valid` of the theorems above holds for every interval that is made by the library's constructors, or computed from such intervals by the operations proved here, any number of times:

```coq
Theorem made_valid :
  (forall x, made x -> valid x) /\ (forall d, dmade d -> valid (itv d)).
```

`made` and `dmade` are inductive predicates on `Interval` and `DecInterval`. Each of their constructors is a place in the library that makes a value other than by an operation, or a proved operation, whose arguments must be `made` or `dmade`. The places that make an `Interval` are `EMPTY`, `const_interval!`, which `ENTIRE` and the other constants of `Interval` are, `zero()`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings, `try_from_ninterval_exact`, which `interval_exact!` calls, and the interval of a `dmade` decorated interval, which `DecInterval::interval` and `From<NInterval>`, which `FromStr` calls, return. Those that make a `DecInterval` are the constants `EMPTY`, `NAI` and `ENTIRE`, those that `def_com!` makes, `const_dec_interval!`, `new`, `set_dec`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings, and `From<DecNInterval>`, which `FromStr` calls. The operations are those above, on `Interval` and on `DecInterval`, and `convex_hull` and `intersection`. A model that returns an `option`, such as that of `x + y`, gives a `made` value for each `Some` that it returns, and that of `mul_rev_to_pair` gives two, one for each output (`made_mul_rev_to_pair1` and `made_mul_rev_to_pair2`). The theorems above show that it returns `Some` for `valid` arguments (for `x * y`, `mul_add`, `recip`, `x / y` and `atan2` of decorated intervals, also given `com_common`, which `ComCommon.v` proves for every decorated interval, and for `powi`, given that the exponent is an `i32`), so the value that the Rust function returns is `made`. Each step can take either value of `has_fma` and `has_maximum`, so `made_valid` holds whatever values a target gives them. A step through a function of `src/elementary.rs` has two constructors. Those named `made_kernel_<f>` and `dmade_kernel_<f>`, such as `made_kernel_exp`, are the library's functions as it is built, which compute their bounds with the dyadic kernel, `kernel_point` of `DyPoint.v`. They take no hypothesis, and cover the library's functions where each call of the kernel returns by one of its rules or by a passing rounding test of Ziv's strategy (see "What is not proved yet"), and, for `cbrt` and `hypot`, whose kernel functions do not use Ziv's strategy, at every argument. The others, such as `made_exp`, are those of the MPFR backend that only the tests compile, and take an MPFR `m` and an exponent range with the properties that its theorems assume, `range_ok`, `get_d_ok` and the function's `<f>_ok`, and for `cos`, `sin` and `tan` both `sin_ok` and `cos_ok`. Both take, for `cos`, `cosh` and `sin`, either value of `first`, for `pow` any values of `first_min` and `first_max`, and for `powi` an exponent `n` with `is_i32 n`. `try_from_ninterval_exact` and `From<DecNInterval>` take any `NInterval`, as any floats that its bounds convert to make a `valid` interval. Every other public function of the library that returns an `Interval` or a `DecInterval` returns one that these make: `FromStr` for `DecInterval` calls `From<DecNInterval>`, `FromStr` for `Interval` returns the interval of that, `_try_from_str_exact`, which `interval_exact!` calls, calls `try_from_ninterval_exact`, the operators on references, such as `&x + &y`, return what `x + y` and the others return, and the compound assignments, such as `x += y`, assign it.

The proof is by induction on `made` and `dmade` together, with a theorem for each of their constructors:

- [`ValidMake.v`](theories/ValidMake.v): the places that make a value other than by an operation. `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, and `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings, call `with_infsup_raw` only for bounds `a <= b` where `a` is not +∞ and `b` is not -∞, which is what `valid` asks for. `const_interval!` does not compile unless its bounds pass the same test (`const_interval_ok`). `EMPTY` holds `[NaN; NaN]`, and `zero()` holds `[+0; +0]`, i.e. `[0, 0]`. `try_from_ninterval_exact` returns an `Err`, or `Ok` of what `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>` returns for the floats that `number_to_f64` computes, or of `EMPTY`, and `From<DecNInterval>` returns `set_dec` of one of these. `new` and `set_dec` keep the interval that they get or hold `Interval::EMPTY`, and the other places that make a `DecInterval` hold one of the intervals above.
- [`ValidOps.v`](theories/ValidOps.v): the proved operations on intervals. `is_hull` and `is_tightest`, which the theorems above conclude of the result, include `valid`, so `floor_valid` takes it out of `floor_correct`, and so on. Each output of `two_output`, which `mul_rev_to_pair_correct` concludes, is `EMPTY` or `is_tightest`, so `mul_rev_to_pair_valid` shows that both are `valid`. An interval of which `cancel_result` holds, as `cancel_minus_correct` and `cancel_plus_correct` conclude, is `valid` (`cancel_result_valid` in `CancelSpec.v`), which gives `cancel_minus_valid` and `cancel_plus_valid`.
- [`ValidSetOp.v`](theories/ValidSetOp.v): `convex_hull_valid` and `intersection_valid`. Both return the empty interval, or the other argument for `convex_hull`, if an argument is empty. Otherwise, `convex_hull` of `[a, b]` and `[c, d]` takes `max` of both lanes, `[max(-a, -c); max(b, d)]`, i.e. `[min(a, c), max(b, d)]`, whose bounds are ordered, as `min(a, c) <= a <= b <= max(b, d)`. `intersection` takes `min` of both lanes, `[max(a, c), min(b, d)]`, and returns the empty interval if the lower bound is above the upper one. On lanes that are finite or +∞, `max1` and `min1` are `Rmax` and `Rmin`, with +∞ above every real, and return such a lane (`max1_bound` and `min1_bound` in `MaxMin.v`), so neither result has a lower bound +∞ or an upper bound -∞.
- [`ValidDecOps.v`](theories/ValidDecOps.v): the proved operations on decorated intervals. Each returns an argument, NaI, or `set_dec` of what the operation on their intervals returns (for `mul_rev_to_pair`, of each of the two intervals that it returns), except `convex_hull` and `intersection`, which return NaI or that interval decorated `trv`.
- [`ValidElem.v`](theories/ValidElem.v): the functions of `src/elementary.rs` above, on intervals and on decorated intervals. `acos_valid` takes `valid` out of `acos_correct`, and so on, and `cos_valid`, `sin_valid` and `tan_valid` take it out of `cos_encloses`, `sin_encloses` and `tan_encloses`, as `encloses` includes it too. The decorated versions return `set_dec` of what the function on their intervals returns, with some decoration (`ve_elem_dec_valid` and `ve_elem_dec_impl_valid`, and for `atan2`, `hypot`, `pow` and `powi`, `dec_atan2_valid`, `dec_hypot_valid`, `dec_pow_valid` and `dec_powi_valid` directly), so `dec_acos_valid` and the others follow as in `ValidDecOps.v`, without `com_common`.

[`MpfrConsistent.v`](theories/MpfrConsistent.v) shows that the hypotheses on MPFR that the theorems above take, which [`Mpfr.v`](theories/Mpfr.v) states (see "What is assumed"), can all hold at once, so the theorems that take them are not true merely because no MPFR could satisfy them. `mpfr_ok emin emax m` is their conjunction: `get_d_ok` and the 23 `<f>_ok` of the functions of `src/elementary.rs`. No theorem takes `mpfr_ok` itself: each keeps its own hypotheses on `m`, which are among these, and those about the functions of `src/elementary.rs` also take `range_ok`. [`MpfrExamples.v`](theories/MpfrExamples.v) checks in the build that the hypotheses on `m` are among these. For each theorem that `Assumptions.v` prints, and each constructor of `made` and `dmade`, that quantifies over an MPFR `m`, an `Example` applies it to the ideal MPFR `ideal emin emax` below, at every exponent range with `range_ok`, and proves from `range_ok` and `ideal_mpfr_ok` alone each of its hypotheses that is `range_ok emin emax` or a conjunct of `mpfr_ok emin emax m`. Its tactic, `mpfr_example`, walks the binders of the theorem up to the first type that is not a product, such as a `let`, an `exists` or a conjunction. It fails on any other hypothesis there (a binder whose type is a `Prop`) whose predicate is `range_ok` or that of a conjunct, or that mentions no variable but `emin`, `emax` and `m`, and keeps the others, such as `valid x`, as hypotheses of the `Example`. `make` fails if one of these theorems or constructors has no `Example`, taking their names from what `Search` prints in `Assumptions.v`. This misses a hypothesis on `m` whose predicate is neither `range_ok` nor that of a conjunct and that also mentions another variable, such as `x`, a hypothesis in the first type that is not a product, such as one after a `let` or in the body of a definition, a binder on `m` whose type is not a `Prop`, a theorem that `Assumptions.v` does not print, or prints by another name than the one that `Search` gives it, one that it prints but that assumes something of an MPFR function without quantifying over an MPFR, a constructor whose name ends in `_ind` or `_sind`, as those of the induction principles of `made` and `dmade` do, a theorem whose name, in `MpfrExamples.v`, refers to another one, such as one that `MpfrExamples.v` declares with the same name, and an `Example` that a `Fail` on an earlier line applies to, as `Fail` has Rocq check that the command after it fails. The generic theorems from which those about the functions are proved, such as `mono_inc_correct`, also take hypotheses on an MPFR function `g`, such as `mpfr_correct`, which those proofs derive from the `<f>_ok` for the field of `m`.

```coq
Theorem mpfr_ok_consistent :
  forall emin emax, range_ok emin emax -> exists m, mpfr_ok emin emax m.

Corollary mpfr_ok_default :
  range_ok (1 - 2 ^ 30) (2 ^ 30 - 1) /\ exists m, mpfr_ok (1 - 2 ^ 30) (2 ^ 30 - 1) m.
```

`mpfr_ok_default` is the instance at MPFR's default exponent range. The `m` is `ideal emin emax` in [`MpfrIdeal.v`](theories/MpfrIdeal.v), an ideal MPFR, and `ideal_mpfr_ok` states that it satisfies `mpfr_ok emin emax` at every range with `range_ok`. Its functions round the point function in the direction asked to `mpfr_format emin emax`, with overflow and underflow as `mpfr_round` has them, and return at the special arguments the values that `Mpfr.v` states. Where `Mpfr.v` states a special value only as a zero, they return one with the sign that MPFR returns. Its `mpfr_get_d` rounds the real of an MPFR number to an `f64` with Flocq's `binary_normalize`. It is a witness, not a model of the MPFR that the Rust code links: where `Mpfr.v` says nothing, such as outside the domain of a function or at a special argument in a direction that `Mpfr.v` does not state, it returns whatever its definition gives, such as NaN or the rounding of the point function, which need not be what MPFR returns. For example, its `pow(-2, -∞)` is +∞, where MPFR's is +0. It is defined with Stdlib's decisions of the order and of the equality of reals (`Rlt_dec`, `Rle_dec` and `Req_dec_T`) and with Flocq's `round`, and `Assumptions.v` shows that the theorems about it depend only on the four axioms below. `ideal_mpfr_ok` follows from a theorem for each part of `mpfr_ok`:

- [`IdealRound.v`](theories/IdealRound.v): `round_mpfr_correct`: `round_mpfr emin emax rnd r`, the rounding that the functions use, is `r` rounded in the direction `rnd` to `mpfr_format emin emax`, as `mpfr_round` says, for every real `r`, if `range_ok emin emax`.
- [`IdealGetD.v`](theories/IdealGetD.v): `ideal_get_d_ok`: the ideal `mpfr_get_d` satisfies `get_d_ok` at every exponent range, from `round_f64_correct`: `round_f64 rnd y` is `y` rounded in the direction `rnd` to `f64_format`, overflow included, for every real `y`.
- [`IdealElem.v`](theories/IdealElem.v): `ideal_acos_ok` and the others, `<f>_ok` for the 19 functions of one argument, from `ideal_fn_correct`, which gives `mpfr_correct` for a function that rounds `f` at the numbers of the domain other than 0, and at the zeros if 0 is in the domain.
- [`IdealPow.v`](theories/IdealPow.v) and [`IdealAtan2.v`](theories/IdealAtan2.v): `ideal_pow_ok`, `ideal_pown_ok` and `ideal_atan2_ok`. Where `pow_ok`, `pown_ok` and `atan2_ok` ask only for a zero, the ideal `pow` and `pow_si` give the zeros of the `mpfr_pow` entry, -0 for `pow_si(-∞, n)` with an odd `n < 0` and +0 elsewhere, and the ideal `atan2(y, +∞)`, for a finite `y`, is the zero with the sign of `y`, as MPFR's is. That is more than they ask, as is the +0 of the ideal `exp`, `exp10` and `exp2` at -∞ in `IdealElem.v`.
- [`IdealHypot.v`](theories/IdealHypot.v): `ideal_hypot_ok`. The ideal `hypot` returns +∞ if one argument is infinite, even if the other is NaN, NaN if one is NaN and the other is not infinite, and otherwise `Rhypot` of the two rounded in the direction asked.

[`PointTheorems.v`](theories/PointTheorems.v) states `exp_correct` for every record `p : point` whose fields satisfy the parts of [`Contract.v`](theories/Contract.v) that it needs. `mpfr_point m` satisfies those parts where `m` satisfies the hypotheses of `Mpfr.v` (see "What is assumed"). `exp2_correct_point`, `exp10_correct_point`, `sinh_correct_point`, `tanh_correct_point`, `cosh_correct_point`, `ln_correct_point`, `log2_correct_point`, `log10_correct_point`, `asinh_correct_point`, `acosh_correct_point`, `atanh_correct_point`, `atan_correct_point`, `asin_correct_point`, `acos_correct_point`, `atan2_correct_point`, `sin_correct_point`, `cos_correct_point`, `tan_correct_point`, `pow_correct_point`, `pown_correct_point`, `cbrt_correct_point` and `hypot_correct_point` state `exp2_correct`, `exp10_correct`, `sinh_correct`, `tanh_correct`, `cosh_correct`, `ln_correct`, `log2_correct`, `log10_correct`, `asinh_correct`, `acosh_correct`, `atanh_correct`, `atan_correct`, `asin_correct`, `acos_correct`, `atan2_correct`, `sin_correct`, `cos_correct`, `tan_correct`, `pow_correct`, `powi_correct`, `cbrt_correct` and `hypot_correct` in the same way, for `DyPoint.v` (above). As `Interval::sin`, `Interval::cos` and `Interval::tan` call both `sin` and `cos` of a record `point` through `quadrant`, `sin_correct_point` and `cos_correct_point` take both `sin_c (p_sin p)` and `cos_c (p_cos p)`, and `tan_correct_point` takes these and `tan_c (p_tan p)`. By the same recipe, [`PointDec.v`](theories/PointDec.v) states the theorems about the decorated elementary functions for a record `point`, `dec_exp_correct_point` to `dec_pown_correct_point`, of which `dec_pown_correct_point` is that of `dec_powi_correct`, and `dec_cbrt_correct_point` and `dec_hypot_correct_point`, for `DyPointDec.v`, and [`PointValid.v`](theories/PointValid.v) those of `ValidElem.v`, `exp_valid_point`, `dec_exp_valid_point` and the other `<f>_valid_point` and `dec_<f>_valid_point`, of which those of `pown` are those of `powi_valid` and `dec_powi_valid`, for the constructors `made_kernel_<f>` and `dmade_kernel_<f>` of `Valid.v`. Apart from `exp_encloses_side` and the other `<f>_encloses_side` of `SideTheorems.v` (above), which take the contracts of `ContractSide.v`, no other theorem about the elementary functions is stated for a record `point`.

```coq
Theorem exp_correct_point :
  forall p, exp_c (p_exp p) ->
  forall x, valid x -> is_tightest (image_dom real_dom exp x) (Interval.exp p x).
```

The files about records `point`, in the order of `_CoqProject`:

- [`Contract.v`](theories/Contract.v): definitions only, `point_correct`, `point_correct2` and `acos_c` to `tanh_c` (see "What is assumed").
- [`ContractSide.v`](theories/ContractSide.v): definitions only, `on_side`, `point_side`, `point_side2` and `acos_s` to `tanh_s` (see "What is assumed").
- [`SideDefs.v`](theories/SideDefs.v): `outward rnd v w`, that the bound `v` is `w` or beyond it in the direction `rnd`, `fld_out`, `fld_out2` and `fld_out_z`, that every value of a field is outward of that of another field at the same arguments, and `wider y y'`, that `y` is valid where `y'` is and contains every real that `y'` contains, so that it encloses every set that `y'` encloses (`encloses_wider`).
- [`OfPointCore.v`](theories/OfPointCore.v): lemmas, none of which `Assumptions.v` prints. `range_ok_f64` is `range_ok (-1073) 1024`, the range of `f64`, at which the least positive and the largest MPFR numbers of 53 bits are those of `f64`. `lift1`, `lift2` and `lift_si` make an MPFR function of a field of a record `point`: they convert the MPFR arguments to `f64`s with the ideal `get_d` toward -∞, apply the field, and make the result an MPFR number with `lift_val` or `with_val`, which the ideal `get_d` in the same direction converts back to that result (`ideal_get_d_lift_val` and `ideal_get_d_with_val`).
- [`OfPoint.v`](theories/OfPoint.v): `of_point p`, the record `mpfr` made of a record `point` `p`, with `mpfr_point_of_point : forall p, mpfr_point (of_point p) = p`, which uses functional extensionality, one of the four axioms (see "What is assumed"), and `of_point_get_d_ok : forall p, get_d_ok (-1073) 1024 (of_point p)`. Its `mpfr_get_d` is that of the ideal MPFR of `MpfrIdeal.v`.
- [`OfPointElem.v`](theories/OfPointElem.v): `of_point_acos_ok` and the others for the 19 functions of one argument, `forall p, <f>_c (p_<f> p) -> <f>_ok (-1073) 1024 (of_point p)`.
- [`OfPointPow.v`](theories/OfPointPow.v): `of_point_atan2_ok`, `of_point_hypot_ok`, `of_point_pow_ok` and `of_point_pown_ok`, of the same shape.
- [`MpfrPoint.v`](theories/MpfrPoint.v): `mpfr_acos_c` and the others for the 23 functions, with the hypotheses in "What is assumed".
- [`PointTheorems.v`](theories/PointTheorems.v): `exp_correct_point` and the twenty-two others above. Each applies the theorem that it restates, such as `exp_correct`, at `of_point p` and the range `[-1073, 1024]`, and rewrites with `mpfr_point_of_point`.
- [`SideMono.v`](theories/SideMono.v): `exp_wider` and the others for the 15 functions whose bounds are fields at the bounds of the argument, or constants: for a record `p` whose field of `f` is outward of that of a record `q`, the interval function of `f` returns for `p` an interval wider than for `q`, at every argument. `wir_wider`: an interval made by `with_infsup_raw` of outward bounds is wider. `fmin_out` and `fmax_out`: the least and the greatest of outward bounds are outward.
- [`SideMonoB.v`](theories/SideMonoB.v): `pow_wider`, `pown_wider`, `atan2_wider`, `sin_wider`, `cos_wider` and `tan_wider`, the same for the functions that take the least or the greatest of fields, return an `option`, or choose their case by `quadrants`, for which the two records must also give the same `quadrant` at every argument (`quadrants_same`).
- [`SideTight.v`](theories/SideTight.v): `side_tight`, `exp_tight_c` to `tanh_tight_c`, `exp_tight_out` to `tanh_tight_out` and `quadrant_tight` above. `on_side_outward`: a value on the side `rnd` of a real is outward of the real rounded in the direction `rnd` (`round_f64`). `exp_c_s` and the others: a field that satisfies `<f>_c` of `Contract.v` satisfies `<f>_s` of `ContractSide.v`, which so asks no more.
- [`SideTheorems.v`](theories/SideTheorems.v): `exp_encloses_side` and the twenty other `<f>_encloses_side` above.
- [`PointDec.v`](theories/PointDec.v): `dec_exp_correct_point` to `dec_pown_correct_point`, `dec_cbrt_correct_point` and `dec_hypot_correct_point`, the 23 theorems about the decorated elementary functions of `Elem.v`, `Cosh.v`, `Atanh.v`, `ClosedDom.v`, `Atan2.v`, `Sin.v`, `Cos.v`, `Tan.v`, `Pow.v`, `Powi.v`, `Cbrt.v` and `Hypot.v`, stated for a record `p` in the same way.
- [`PointValid.v`](theories/PointValid.v): `exp_valid_point`, `dec_exp_valid_point` and the 44 others, the theorems of `ValidElem.v` stated for a record `p` in the same way.

On the way, the other files prove:

- [`Encoding.v`](theories/Encoding.v): `is_finite_eq`, i.e. `is_finite` agrees with Flocq's, `abs_B2R_lt_exponent`, i.e. `|x| < 2^(exponent(x) + 1)`, and `bpow_exponent_le_abs_B2R`, i.e. `2^exponent(x) <= |x|` if `x` is normal. `step_up_Bsucc` shows that `step_up` is Flocq's `Bsucc` (IEEE 754 `nextUp`) on finite floats.
