The theorems about `kernel_point_rust` of `DyPointSide.v` cover the calls that reach the cap too, with intervals that contain the range in place of the tightest ones, and say nothing of the Rust at the arguments at which a call of the kernel panics. The hypotheses of `Mpfr.v` (`range_ok`, `get_d_ok` and the `<f>_ok`), `MpfrPoint.v` and `MpfrConsistent.v`, and the theorems about MPFR functions `m`, describe the MPFR backend that only the tests compile. The theorems about `kernel_point` take none of the hypotheses of `Mpfr.v`: their statements and the contracts that they rest on use only its definitions, such as that of rounding, `mpfr_round`, with which `Contract.v` states its contracts.

[`Mpfr.v`](theories/Mpfr.v) states what the theorems about the functions of `src/elementary.rs` with the MPFR backend assume of MPFR, and has to be checked by reading against the MPFR manual (of MPFR 4.2.2, which `gmp-mpfr-sys` 1.7.1 bundles), as `Model.v` is against the Rust code. `mpfr_ok_consistent` in `MpfrConsistent.v` shows that the hypotheses can all hold at once, for an ideal MPFR (see "What is proved"), but not that the MPFR that the Rust code links satisfies them. The unit tests in [`src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs`](../src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs), which run on 64-bit Linux and macOS, call the linked MPFR through the code's own calls (the arms of `mpfr_fn!`, `mpfr_fn2!` and `mpfr_fn_si!` that give the MPFR number before `get_d`, and `get_d` itself, in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`) on every special value that the hypotheses state, in each direction they state it for, at a few arguments where a clause holds for many, on `mpfr_get_d` near the overflow and subnormal thresholds of `f64`, also at the exponent range of `f64` that `with_parse_range` there sets, and on results that overflow or underflow MPFR's exponent range, and check the exact results. Where the hypotheses state a special value only as a zero, of either sign, the tests also check the sign of that zero, which is more than the hypotheses assume. That tests the linked MPFR at these points only. The test `round_rational_oracle` there checks `round_rational` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which, like `with_parse_range`, only the tests compile, against what the MPFR manual says of `mpfr_set_q`, `mpfr_subnormalize` and `mpfr_get_d`, with exact and inexact rationals of both signs near the subnormal and overflow thresholds: `src/parse/diff_tests.rs` tests `rational_to_f64` against `round_rational`, and no theorem assumes anything of `round_rational`. Every hypothesis is an argument of the theorems, not an axiom:

- `range_ok emin emax`: MPFR's exponent range `[emin, emax]` contains `[-1073, 1024]`, so that every finite `f64` is an MPFR number of 53 bits. Neither `src/elementary.rs` nor the macros of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which only the tests compile, set the range, which in an MPFR built thread-safe is that of the calling thread. MPFR's default range, `[-(2^30 - 1), 2^30 - 1]` (`MPFR_EMIN_DEFAULT` and `MPFR_EMAX_DEFAULT` in `mpfr.h`), contains it, but a test that narrows the range with `mpfr_set_emin` or `mpfr_set_emax` around a call of the MPFR backend is not covered.
- `mpfr_format emin emax`: the reals that an MPFR number of 53 bits holds, 0 and `m × 2^e` with a significand `m` of 53 bits, `1/2 <= |m| < 1` and `emin <= e <= emax`, as MPFR has no subnormals (§4.2 "Nomenclature and Types" and `mpfr_get_emin` in §5.13). `f64_format` is the reals of finite `f64`s.
- `mpfr_round F rnd r v`: toward -∞, `v` is the largest element of `F` at or below `r`, or -∞ if there is none, and toward +∞ the least at or above `r`, or +∞. This is §4.4 "Rounding", which rounds toward -∞ to the number closest to `r` at or below it, and toward +∞ at or above it, read with the infinities among the numbers and the exponent range of §4.2, so that it covers overflow and underflow. §4.6 "Exceptions" says when these occur, but gives the result only for an overflow rounded to nearest, so the results toward -∞ and +∞, such as the largest MPFR number for a positive overflow rounded down, are read into §4.4. A result 0 may be a zero of either sign, as the theorems do not use it, and a result other than 0 is an `MNum`.
- `get_d_ok`: `mpfr_get_d` converts an MPFR number of 53 bits other than 0 to the `f64` that rounds it in the direction `rnd`, including overflow and the subnormal range of `f64`, a zero to a zero, and an infinity to the infinity of the same sign (`mpfr_get_d` in §5.4 "Conversion Functions"). The entry says only that it converts "using the rounding mode rnd", so that this rounding is to binary64 with its overflow and its subnormals, as IEEE 754 rounds, is read into it.
- `mpfr_correct emin emax D f g`: for a finite `f64` `x` whose real is in the domain `D`, `g rnd (with_val x)` is `f(x)` rounded in the direction `rnd` to an MPFR number of 53 bits. MPFR rounds every function correctly on its domain (§4.4, and §4.5 "Floating-Point Values on Special Numbers": "When the input point is in the domain of the mathematical function, the result is rounded as described in Rounding"). The entries of the functions in §5.7 "Transcendental Functions" say which mathematical function each computes, and `Elementary.v` defines it.
- `<f>_ok`, such as `exp_ok`, is `mpfr_correct` for the function, its point function and its domain, and the values at the special arguments that `src/elementary.rs` passes it, which follow from the rules of §4.5 for a point outside the domain but in its closure, the limit, and from the entries of §5.7: `exp`, `exp2` and `exp10` give +0 at -∞ and +∞ at +∞, `ln`, `log2` and `log10` give -∞ at ±0 and +∞ at +∞, `sinh`, `asinh` and `cbrt` give -∞ and +∞ at -∞ and +∞, `tanh` gives -1 and 1 at -∞ and +∞, `atan` gives `-π/2` rounded down and `π/2` rounded up, `cosh` gives +∞ at either infinity, `acosh` gives +∞ at +∞, and `atanh` gives -∞ at -1 and +∞ at 1. For the one zero among these values, +0 at -∞, `exp_ok`, `exp2_ok` and `exp10_ok` assume only a zero, of either sign: the code passes the result of each of these MPFR functions only to `mpfr_get_d`, and `get_d_ok` says only that it converts a zero to a zero, so the theorems cannot use the sign. The same holds for the zeros of `pow_ok`, `pown_ok` and `atan2_ok` below. Only the directions in which the code passes these arguments are assumed: `f_rd` gets -∞, -1 or a zero, and `f_ru` +∞, 1, or, for `cosh`, -∞. `acos_ok` and `asin_ok` are `mpfr_correct` alone, as the code passes these functions only reals of `[-1, 1]`, and so are `cos_ok`, `sin_ok` and `tan_ok`, as the code passes `cos`, `sin` and `tan` only finite floats. The domain in `tan_ok` is `tan_dom`.
- `mpfr_correct2 emin emax D f g` is `mpfr_correct` for an MPFR function `g` of two numbers, a function `f` of two reals and a domain `D` of points of R². `pow_ok` is `mpfr_correct2` for `mpfr_pow`, `Rpow` and `pow_dom`, which also covers `pow(-0, t)` for `t > 0`, as the real of -0 is 0, and a zero result may have either sign. It adds the values that the `mpfr_pow` entry of §5.7 gives at the points other than these that `Interval::pow` passes: `pow(+0, t)` is +∞ for `t` below 0 or -∞ and +0 for `t` = +∞, `pow(r, ±0)` is 1 for `r` = +0 or +∞, `pow(+1, ±∞)` is 1, `pow(r, -∞)` is +∞ for `0 < r < 1` and +0 for `r > 1`, `pow(r, +∞)` is +0 for `0 < r < 1` and +∞ for `r > 1`, and `pow(+∞, t)` is +0 for `t` below 0 or -∞ and +∞ for `t` above 0 or +∞. Where the entry gives +0, `pow_ok` assumes only a zero, of either sign. `pown_ok` states the same of `mpfr_pow_si`, which that entry covers too: for an `n` with `is_i32 n` and a finite `x` whose real is in `pown_dom n`, `mpfr_pow_si rnd (with_val x) n` is `powerRZ x n` rounded in the direction `rnd`, and at the other points that `Interval::powi` passes, `pow_si(±0, n)` for `n < 0` is +∞ for an even `n` and the infinity with the sign of the zero for an odd one, `pow_si(+∞, n)` is +0 for `n < 0`, 1 for `n = 0` and +∞ for `n > 0`, and `pow_si(-∞, n)` for an odd `n` is -0 for `n < 0` and -∞ for `n > 0`, except that where the entry gives +0 or -0, `pown_ok` assumes only a zero. Unlike the special values of `<f>_ok` above, which are assumed only in the directions in which the code passes their arguments, these are assumed for both directions, as MPFR returns each of them exactly, whatever the direction. `is_i32 n` in `Model.v` says that `n` is in the range of `i32`, which `c_long` contains on every platform. `atan2_ok` is `mpfr_correct2` for `mpfr_atan2`, `Ratan2` and the points `(y, x)` with `y` other than 0 or `x` above 0, which covers `atan2(±0, x)` for `x > 0`, and adds the values that the `mpfr_atan2` entry of §5.7 gives at the points with one infinity that `Interval::atan2` passes, except that at `(y, +∞)` it assumes only a zero, of either sign, as `exp_ok` does at -∞: `atan2(y, +∞)` is a zero for a finite `y`, `atan2(y, -∞)` is π for a finite `y > 0` and -π for a finite `y < 0`, and `atan2(+∞, x)` and `atan2(-∞, x)` are π/2 and -π/2 for a finite `x`. These are assumed for both directions: the zeros are exact, and π, -π, π/2 and -π/2 are rounded in the direction `rnd`. The entry gives `atan2(y, +∞)` as +0 for a finite `y > 0` and -0 for a finite `y < 0`. For `y` = ±0, it gives ±0 by its `atan2(±0, x) = ±0` for `x > 0` if that `x` can be +∞. If it cannot, no bullet of the entry names the point, and the rule of §4.5 for a point outside the domain but in its closure gives the limit, 0. The wording favours the first reading: the bullets with one infinite argument say "for finite x", "for finite y > 0" or "for finite y < 0", and those of `atan2(±0, x) = ±0` say only "for x > 0". The entry cites the ISO C99 and IEEE 754 standards for its special values. C99 gives those of `atan2` in its Annex F, whose F.9.1.4, in the draft N1256 of C99 (ISO/IEC 9899:TC3), says `atan2(±0, x) returns ±0 for x > 0` and `atan2(±y, +∞) returns ±0 for finite y > 0`, with the same two readings and the same contrast. MPFR's `src/atan2.c` lists the bullets of F.9.1.4, with the same values and conditions, in a comment (l.66-79 in MPFR 4.2.2) that names no source, in another order, after the footnote of their first bullet. The unit test `atan2_ok` in `src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs` also checks, at a few `y`, that the linked MPFR returns the zero with the sign of `y`, as `src/atan2.c` does for every finite `y`. The domain of `mpfr_correct2` leaves out the points with `y = 0` and `x <= 0`, so also the half-line `y = 0`, `x < 0` of `atan2_dom`, as the entry gives `atan2(±0, x) = ±π` for `x < 0`, by the sign of the zero, which no function of the reals gives. The code never passes them (see `atan2_correct` above). The values that `pow_ok` and `atan2_ok` add at a finite argument other than 0, such as `pow(r, -∞)` for `0 < r < 1` or `atan2(y, -∞)` for `y > 0`, are stated, as `mpfr_correct2` is, only where that argument is the real of a finite `f64` (`with_val`), as the code passes it, not at every real. `hypot_ok` is `mpfr_correct2` for `mpfr_hypot`, `Rhypot` and all of R², and adds that `hypot(+∞, y)` and `hypot(x, +∞)` rounded up are +∞ for every `f64` `y` and `x`, NaN and the infinities included, as the `mpfr_hypot` entry of §5.5 "Arithmetic Functions" says that it returns +∞ if one argument is an infinity, even if the other is NaN. `Interval::hypot` passes +∞ only to `hypot_ru`, as an upper bound of `|x|` or `|y|`.

[`Contract.v`](theories/Contract.v) states what each field of a record `point` must do at the level of `f64`s, and has to be checked by reading, as `Mpfr.v` is. `point_correct` and `point_correct2` are the counterparts of `mpfr_correct` and `mpfr_correct2`: for finite `f64`s whose reals are in the domain, the field rounds the function of those reals in the direction `rnd` to an `f64`. Each `<f>_c`, such as `exp_c`, transcribes `<f>_ok` from MPFR numbers to `f64`s: an argument `with_val x` becomes `x`, a zero or an infinity of MPFR the `f64` zero or infinity of the same sign, the MPFR number 1 or -1 the `f64` 1 or -1, a result rounded to an MPFR number of 53 bits one rounded to an `f64`, and a special value that `<f>_ok` states only as a zero, of some sign, stays only a zero. [`MpfrPoint.v`](theories/MpfrPoint.v) shows that `mpfr_point m` does what `Contract.v` states where `m` satisfies `Mpfr.v`'s hypotheses: `mpfr_<f>_c : forall emin emax m, range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> <f>_ok emin emax m -> <f>_c (p_<f> (mpfr_point m))`. Of the theorems about the elementary functions, only `exp_correct_point`, `exp2_correct_point`, `exp10_correct_point`, `sinh_correct_point`, `tanh_correct_point`, `cosh_correct_point`, `ln_correct_point`, `log2_correct_point`, `log10_correct_point`, `asinh_correct_point`, `acosh_correct_point`, `atanh_correct_point`, `atan_correct_point`, `asin_correct_point`, `acos_correct_point`, `atan2_correct_point`, `sin_correct_point`, `cos_correct_point`, `tan_correct_point`, `pow_correct_point`, `pown_correct_point`, `cbrt_correct_point` and `hypot_correct_point` of `PointTheorems.v`, `dec_exp_correct_point` to `dec_pown_correct_point`, `dec_cbrt_correct_point` and `dec_hypot_correct_point` of `PointDec.v`, and `exp_valid_point`, `dec_exp_valid_point` and the other `<f>_valid_point` and `dec_<f>_valid_point` of `PointValid.v` are stated for a record `p : point`, with the hypothesis `<f>_c (p_<f> p)`, such as `exp_c (p_exp p)` and `pown_c (p_pown p)`, and also `sin_c (p_sin p)` and `cos_c (p_cos p)` for those of sin, cos and tan, and they have no hypothesis about MPFR. `kernel_exp_correct` and the other `kernel_<f>_correct` of `DyPoint.v`, `kernel_dec_exp_correct` and the other `kernel_dec_<f>_correct` of `DyPointDec.v`, and the constructors `made_kernel_<f>` and `dmade_kernel_<f>` of `Valid.v` are stated for `kernel_point`, with no hypothesis about the kernel or MPFR. Besides these, `exp_encloses_side` and the other `<f>_encloses_side` of `SideTheorems.v` are stated for a record `p : point` with the hypotheses `<f>_s (p_<f> p)` of `ContractSide.v` in place of `<f>_c (p_<f> p)`, and `kernel_exp_encloses` and the other `kernel_<f>_encloses` of `DyPointSide.v` for `kernel_point_rust`, with no hypothesis. The others are stated for MPFR functions `m`, through `mpfr_point m`, and what they assume of `m` is stated in `Mpfr.v`; they are about the MPFR backend, which only the tests compile.

[`ContractSide.v`](theories/ContractSide.v) states a weaker contract for each field of a record `point` other than those of `cbrt` and `hypot`, which the functions of the kernel satisfy also where `ziv` reaches its cap, and has to be checked by reading, as `Contract.v` is. `on_side rnd r v`: `v` is at or below `r` for `RNDD` and at or above `r` for `RNDU`, it is not a NaN, and it is an infinity only of that side, -∞ for `RNDD` and +∞ for `RNDU`. `point_side` and `point_side2` are the counterparts of `point_correct` and `point_correct2`: for finite `f64`s whose reals are in the domain, the field is on the side `rnd` of the function of those reals. Each `<f>_s`, such as `exp_s`, is `<f>_c` of `Contract.v` with `point_side` in place of `point_correct` and `point_side2` in place of `point_correct2`, and the special values copied unchanged. There is none for `cbrt` and `hypot`, as their functions of the kernel do not use `ziv` (above). `sin_s` and `cos_s` also ask that the field of `sin` rounded down is below 0 exactly where sin is, and the field of `cos` rounded up above 0 exactly where cos is, at every finite argument, the signs that `quadrant` reads. `exp_c_s` and the others of `SideTight.v` check that each `<f>_s` asks no more than `<f>_c`.

[`NearF64.v`](theories/NearF64.v) states `near_round`, the contract of the functions of the kernel rounded to nearest and of the `_rn` functions of `inari::cr`, and has to be checked by reading, as `Contract.v` is. `near_round r v` says that `v` is the real `r` rounded to the nearest `f64`, ties to even, as IEEE 754's roundTiesToEven rounds it, and as MPFR's `RNDN` does in the exponent range of `f64` with `mpfr_subnormalize`: an infinity of the sign of `r` from 2^1024 - 2^970 up in magnitude, which is `f64::MAX` plus half of its ulp, where the tie goes to 2^1024, and below it the finite `f64` whose real is Flocq's `round radix2 (FLT_exp (-1074) 53) ZnearestE r`, which, where it is a zero and `r` is not 0, has the sign of `r`. At `r = 0` it allows either zero, as `mpfr_round` does. Like the theorems about `kernel_point`, the theorems of the functions rounded to nearest take none of the hypotheses of `Mpfr.v`. They are about finite arguments, so the values at the infinities and at NaN are not stated (see "What is not proved yet").

[`Elementary.v`](theories/Elementary.v) defines the point functions that are not in Stdlib, by the usual formulas: `Rexp2` and `Rexp10` as `Rpower 2` and `Rpower 10`, `Rlog2` and `Rlog10` as `Rlog 2` and `Rlog 10`, i.e. `ln r / ln 2` and `ln r / ln 10`, `Racosh r` as `ln (r + sqrt (r * r - 1))` and `Ratanh r` as `ln ((1 + r) / (1 - r)) / 2`, and the domains `real_dom`, all of R, `log_dom`, `(0, +∞)`, `asin_dom` and `acos_dom`, `[-1, 1]`, `acosh_dom`, `[1, +∞)`, `atanh_dom`, `(-1, 1)`, and `tan_dom`, R without the odd multiples of π/2, those of Table 9.1 of the draft P1788/D9.5. It defines `Rpow r t`, IEEE 1788's `pow`, as `Rpower r t`, i.e. `exp(t ln r)`, for `r > 0` and 0 otherwise, with the domain `pow_dom` of that table, the points `(r, t)` with `r > 0`, or with `r = 0` and `t > 0`, and the domain `pown_dom n` of `pown(x, n)`, all of R for `n >= 0` and R without 0 for `n < 0`. It defines `Ratan2 r t`, IEEE 1788's `atan2(y, x)` with `r` as `y` and `t` as `x`, by the usual formula: `atan(r / t)` for `t > 0`, that plus π for `t < 0` and `r >= 0`, and minus π for `t < 0` and `r < 0`, and π/2 or -π/2 by the sign of `r` for `t = 0`, with the domain `atan2_dom` of that table, R² without `(0, 0)`. The table defines `atan2(y, x)` as the principal value of the argument of `(x, y)`, and `Ratan2_range`, `Ratan2_polar` and `Ratan2_polar_unique` in `AtanReal.v` show that `Ratan2` is that angle, so the formula can be checked against the table's definition by those lemmas as well as by reading. Stdlib's `exp`, `ln`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `arcsinh`, `atan`, `asin` and `acos` are IEEE 1788's `exp`, `log`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `asinh`, `atan`, `asin` and `acos`, and Stdlib's `powerRZ r n`, as a function of `r`, is IEEE 1788's `pown(x, n)`. It defines the cube root `Rcbrt r`, `rootn(x, 3)` of the recommended functions of IEEE 1788 (Table 10.5 of the draft P1788/D9.5), on all of R, as `Rpower r (/ 3)`, i.e. `exp(ln r / 3)`, for `r > 0`, 0 for `r = 0` and `- Rpower (- r) (/ 3)` for `r < 0`, and `Rcbrt_cube` and `Rcbrt_unique` in `RootReal.v` show that it is the one real whose cube is `r`, so it can be checked by those lemmas as well as by reading. It defines `Rhypot r t`, IEEE 1788's `hypot(x, y)` of the same table, on all of R², as Stdlib's `sqrt (r * r + t * t)`, which the theorems of `DyHypot.v` state unfolded. Like `Ieee1788.v`, it has to be checked by reading. A function defined only on a domain is a function from R to R, or from R² to R for `pow` and `atan2`, whose values outside the domain the theorems do not use.

[`Ieee1788.v`](theories/Ieee1788.v) states what IEEE Std 1788-2015 asks of an interval version of a function: the hull of the image (`is_hull`), or the tightest interval that contains it (`is_tightest`) where its bounds need not be floats, or an interval that contains it (`encloses`), the accuracy that IEEE 1788 calls valid, which `cos_encloses`, `sin_encloses` and `tan_encloses` state on their own, and the properties of the decorations. It and `Elementary.v` are the only files that have to be checked against the standard by reading, its `two_output` against the doc comment of `Interval::mul_rev_to_pair`, and its `cancel_result` against that of `Interval::cancel_minus`. For `com`, the standard asks for the computed interval to be bounded. `p`, for functions of one real, asks for the image instead. `is_hull_bounded` in `Integer.v` shows that the two are the same for a hull, which each function stated with `p` returns. `p2`, for functions of two reals, asks for the computed interval, as the standard does, as `x + y` can overflow where the image is bounded. Stdlib's `continuity_pt` and `continue_in` are for functions of one real, so for functions of two, `Ieee1788.v` defines continuity on R² itself, by ε and δ with the norm `max(|r|, |t|)` (`continue_in2`, `continuity2_pt`). It also defines the image of a box (`image2`) and the decorations of a function of two reals (`p2`, `strongest2`). For a function of one real that is defined only on a set of reals, its domain, such as `sqrt` on `[0, +∞)` (`sqrt_dom`), it defines the image of the reals of an interval that are in the domain (`image_dom`) and the decorations (`p_dom`, `strongest_dom`). `def`, `dac` and `com` ask for the interval to be a nonempty subset of the domain, and `com` for continuity at each of its points relative to the domain, as IEEE 1788 counts `sqrt` as continuous at 0. For `com`, `p_dom`, like `p2`, asks for the computed interval to be bounded. `sqr` is stated with these too, with the domain `sqr_dom`, which is all of R, because a square can overflow where the image is bounded, and so is `recip`, with the domain `recip_dom`, the reals other than 0. For a function of two reals whose domain is a product `D1 × D2` of two sets of reals, such as `/` on `div_dom1 × div_dom2`, i.e. R × (R ∖ {0}), it defines the image of the points of a box that are in the domain (`image2_dom`) and the decorations (`p2_dom`, `strongest2_dom`), as `image_dom`, `p_dom` and `strongest_dom` do for one real. A box is a nonempty subset of such a domain exactly when each of its sides is a nonempty subset of the matching factor, and `continue_in2` already takes a product of two sets, so `p2_dom` can state continuity relative to the domain as it states continuity relative to a box. A product suffices for `/`. For a function of two reals whose domain is any set `D` of points of R², such as `pow` and `atan2`, whose domains `pow_dom` and `atan2_dom` are not products, it defines the image of the points of a box that are in `D` (`image2_set`), continuity at a point relative to `D` (`continue_in2_set`), and the decorations (`p2_set`, `strongest2_set`), where `dac` asks for the restriction of the function to the box to be continuous, and `com` for continuity at each point of the box relative to `D`, as `p2_dom` does for a product. [`Dom2.v`](theories/Dom2.v) shows that for a product `D1 × D2` these are `image2_dom`, `continue_in2`, `p2_dom` and `strongest2_dom`, and [`DecBinary.v`](theories/DecBinary.v) that for all of R² `image2_set`, `p2_set` and `strongest2_set` are `image2`, `p2` and `strongest2`. For a function of three reals that is defined on all of R³, such as `fma`, which is `Rfma`, `r * t + u`, it defines continuity on R³ as on R², with the norm `max(|r|, |t|, |u|)` (`continue_in3`, `continuity3_pt`), the image of a box `x × y × z` (`image3`), and the decorations (`p3`, `strongest3`), where `com`, as in `p2`, asks for the computed interval to be bounded. For the constant function `c()` of a real `c`, a function of no arguments, it defines the decorations (`p0`, `strongest0`). R⁰ has one point, the empty tuple, so its only box is R⁰ itself, which is nonempty, bounded and the domain of `c()`, and `c()` is continuous on it. So `trv`, `def` and `dac` always hold, `com` asks for the computed interval to be bounded, as Annex B1 of the draft P1788/D9.5 says for a constant, and `ill` never holds, as it asks for an empty domain, which only NaN, the function of no arguments that is defined nowhere, has. For two-output division, `mulRevToPair(b, c)`, it defines the set `mul_rev b c` of reals `z` with `t * z` in `c` for some `t` in `b`, the convex sets of reals (`convex`), the connected components of a set of reals, which are its maximal nonempty convex subsets (`component`), and the outputs that the doc comment of `Interval::mul_rev_to_pair` describes for a set (`two_output`): both `EMPTY` for an empty set, the tightest interval that contains its only component and `EMPTY` for a set with one component, and the tightest intervals that contain its two components, the lower one first, for a set with two. For cancellative subtraction, `cancelMinus(x, y)`, it defines, for sets of reals `X` and `Y`, `Y + Z ⊇ X` (`sum_superset`), when cancelMinus has a value (`cancel_defined`): for an empty `X` and a bounded `Y`, and for `X = [a, b]` and `Y = [c, d]` with `d - c <= b - a`, and the result that the doc comment of `Interval::cancel_minus` describes (`cancel_result`): if cancelMinus has a value, the `valid` interval `z` with `Y + z ⊇ X` that every other `valid` interval with this property contains, and `ENTIRE` otherwise. `X` and `Y` are sets rather than intervals so that `cancelPlus(x, y)`, which is `cancelMinus(x, -y)`, is stated with the negations of the reals of `y` rather than with the model of `-y`. For interval literals, it defines the set of reals that an `NInterval` denotes (`ninterval_set`), with `above_num` and `below_num` for the reals at or above a lower bound and at or below an upper bound, where -∞ and +∞ bound nothing on their side and a lower bound +∞ or an upper bound -∞ leaves no real, as in `NInterval::EMPTY`. The theorems state textToInterval's result as the tightest interval that contains that set, the hull that §12.12.7 of the draft P1788/D9.5 asks for in an IEEE 754 binary64 type.

The theorems depend on four axioms. Three come from Stdlib's real numbers: `sig_forall_dec`, `sig_not_dec` and `functional_extensionality_dep`. The fourth is excluded middle (`classic`), which Flocq uses. The bounds `cm_gappa` and `s3_gappa` of [`FastTrigGappa.v`](theories/FastTrigGappa.v) are proved by the `gappa` tactic of coq-gappa, which runs the Gappa program on the goal and turns its certificate into a proof term built from coq-gappa's lemmas, which Rocq's kernel checks at `Qed`, so the Gappa program is not trusted and these proofs rest on the same four axioms. `make` fails if [`Assumptions.v`](Assumptions.v) finds any other axiom, e.g. a proof left `Admitted`. It does not print the `Example`s of `MpfrExamples.v`, each of which applies a theorem that it prints, or a constructor of `made` or `dmade`, of which it prints `made_valid`, to `ideal emin emax` and to proofs from `range_ok` and `ideal_mpfr_ok`, which it prints too.

## What is not proved yet

- The SIMD backends that vectorize the same algorithm, such as `src/simd/x86_64/lanewise.rs` and `src/simd/wasm32.rs`. If the fast path rejects either lane, `mul_ru` of the `fma`, `sse42` and `wasm32` backends calls `mul_ru_tiny`, which rounds a product below `2^-968` as `mul1_ru_tiny` does, but differs from it in three ways. It computes three values in both lanes and selects one of them in each lane: the result for a zero `RN(x * y)`, the fast path's rounding of one product, which is `x * y` in a lane that the fast path accepts and the scaled product in one that it rejects, and that rounding divided by `2^128` and rounded upward. `mul1_ru_tiny` computes only those that its lane needs. It tells the sign of a zero `RN(x * y)` by comparing its bit pattern with that of +0 as signed integers. And it does not choose the lanes by the exponent of `RN(x * y)`, as `mul1_ru_tiny` does: it accepts a rejected lane if `RN(x * y)` is zero or `two_prod_is_exact` holds for the scaled product, which it checks at run time, and otherwise has the portable backend's `mul_ru` recompute both lanes.
- The other backends' versions of the functions that `abs`, `cancel_minus`, `cancel_plus`, `floor`, `ceil`, `max`, `min`, `mul_add`, `mul_rev_to_pair`, `recip`, `round`, `round_ties_even`, `sign`, `sqr`, `sqrt`, `trunc`, `-x`, `x + y`, `x - y`, `x * y`, `x / y`, `convex_hull` and `intersection` of intervals, the 23 functions of `src/elementary.rs` above, of which `cos`, `sin` and `tan` also call `x / y` and `floor`, `powi` and `hypot` `abs`, and `atan2` `classify` and `-x`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings, and `From<DecNInterval>`, `From<NInterval>` and `try_from_ninterval_exact`, which call `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, and the first two also `set_dec`, call: `floor`, `ceil`, `round`, `round_ties_even`, `trunc` and `neg0`, such as `_mm_floor_pd` on x86-64, `splat`, `gt`, `lt`, `and` and `or` in `sign`, `ge`, `le`, `bitmask`, `splat`, `max`, `swap` and `shuffle02` in `abs` and `classify`, `max`, `maximum`, `min`, `minimum` and `shuffle03` in `max` and `min`, `swap` in `-x` and `x - y`, `add_ru` in `x + y` and `x - y`, `max`, `mul_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `x * y`, all but `shuffle13` also in `sqr`, `max`, `mul_add_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13` and `swap` in `mul_add`, `sqrt1_ru`, `sqrt_rd_ru` and `neg0` in `sqrt`, `div_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle03`, `splat` and `swap` in `x / y`, `div_ru`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `recip`, `div_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle03`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `mul_rev_to_pair`, `sub_ru`, `hadd_rn`, `extract`, `constant`, `max`, `min`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `sub_rn` and `add_rn` in `cancel_minus`, which `cancel_plus` calls on the result of `-x`, `le`, `all`, `neg0` and `splat` in `contains`, which the decorated `recip`, `x / y` and `mul_rev_to_pair` call, `lt`, `all`, `splat`, `extract0` and `extract1` in `is_empty`, `is_singleton`, `is_common_interval`, `inf_raw` and `sup_raw`, `max` in `convex_hull`, `min` in `intersection`, which `acos`, `acosh`, `asin`, `atanh`, the logarithms and `pow` call, `eq`, `all`, `splat`, `shuffle02` and `shuffle13` in `interior`, which `atanh`, the logarithms and `pow` call, `le` and `all` in `subset`, which `acos`, `acosh`, `asin` and `pow` call, and `constant` in `with_infsup_raw`, which `sqrt`, the functions of `src/elementary.rs`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, and `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings call. The roundings are meant to be the same IEEE 754 operations in every backend, but the theorems are proved only for the portable backend's. `rational_to_f64` in `src/parse.rs`, which those three conversions call through `number_to_f64`, calls the portable backend's `round_up_exact` on every backend, as `src/simd.rs` imports that on each, so its theorem holds on every backend. So do those of `to_f64` and `to_f64_near` of `src/dyadic.rs`. `sqrt_rd` and `sqrt_ru` of `inari::cr` call the backend's `sqrt1_rd` and `sqrt1_ru`, whose theorems in `CrRoot.v` are those of the portable backend, which the x86-64 backends but `avx512f`, and wasm32, call for them; the `avx512f` and `aarch64` backends compute them with `vsqrtsd` and `fsqrt` rounded toward -∞ and +∞, which is not proved.
- That Ziv's strategy in `src/dyadic.rs` returns the correct rounding, that is, that a rounding test passes by its cap of 4,096 bits, where it returns instead the bound of its enclosure on the side of the rounding, rounded outward: `ziv_strict_correct` is about the number that it returns where a test passes, `ziv_side` shows only that it returns a value on the correct side, and `ziv_at_passes` shows only that a level passes once its enclosure is narrow enough. So neither is it proved that `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10`, `sinh`, `cosh` and `tanh` of `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, `ln`, `log2`, `log10`, `asinh`, `acosh` and `atanh` of `src/dyadic/log.rs`, `atan`, `atan2`, `asin` and `acos` of `src/dyadic/atan.rs`, `sin`, `cos` and `tan` of `src/dyadic/trig.rs`, or `pow` and `pown` of `src/dyadic/pow.rs`, outside the arguments at which their exact results are `f64`s (below), return their results correctly rounded rather than the value at the cap, nor that they do not panic: their theorems of correct rounding cover the calls at which a rule or a fast path settles the argument without calling `ziv` or a test of `ziv` passes, and those of the side the calls that do not panic (see "What is assumed"). At every finite argument in its domain at which its exact result is an `f64`, each of these 21 functions returns that `f64` in both directions, where it is 0 the zero named in "What is proved", by a rule, by `exact` for x^y, or by `ziv` at an exact enclosure (`exp_dir_exact` and the others in "What is proved", with x > 0 for x^y and x other than 0 for x^n), so that it does not reach the cap at those arguments; for `sinh`, `cosh`, `asinh` and `acosh`, this is proved under the hypothesis `exp_not_quadratic`, which follows from the theorem of Hermite and Lindemann, not proved here. At each other finite argument that no rule settles, the result is not an `f64`, so that `ziv_at_passes` applies, and it would take that the enclosures are narrow enough by 4,096 bits. Nor is it proved that `tiny_quotient` and `midpoint_quotient`, the rules of `atan2` for `x > 0`, return every quotient that they look for, or the same as the division of `Dyadic`s that they make only at `x >= 2^996`: `tiny_quotient_correct` and `midpoint_quotient_correct` show only that what they return is right, and the tests check the rest, against that division and against an integer oracle (`src/dyadic/atan/tests/exact_div.rs`). An argument that they miss is left to the fast path and to `ziv` or `ziv_near`. That the result is an `f64` only at the arguments named in "What is proved", x = ±0 for most functions, follows for the 19 functions other than x^y and x^n, as in the proofs of those theorems, from `f64_rational` with `exp_irrational`, `cos_irrational`, `pow2_rational` and `pow10_rational`, and with `exp_not_quadratic` for `sinh`, `cosh`, `asinh` and `acosh`, though no theorem here states it for each function; `pi_times`, which `atan` and `atan2` call, does not call `ziv` at the `c` that they pass, ±1/4, ±1/2, ±3/4 and ±1, whose roundings [`AtanPiConst.v`](theories/AtanPiConst.v) proves, and at any other finite `c` except 0, `c π` is not an `f64`, by `pi_irrational` above, as π times a rational other than 0 is irrational. For the trigonometric functions, the width of the enclosures at those arguments comes from that of `reduce`. `km_distance` shows that no finite `f64` lies within 2^-61 of a multiple of π/2 other than 0 (the least distance, about 2^-60.9, is cited, not proved), and with it `tan_div_pos` that the enclosure by which `tan` divides lies above 0 wherever the operations that compute it return, so that `Encl::div` does not panic there for a divisor that contains 0 (see "What is proved"). They also need that `reduce` passes its `assert!`, `|ρ| <= 1`, which holds as `reduce` chooses `k` so that `|ρ|` is at most π/4 plus the width of the reduction (`reduce_le` bounds ρ by 4/5 only where `reduce` returns), and that the operations of `tan` before `Encl::div`, among them the square root that gives cos ρ, do not panic. Other than the theorems of exact results and of irrationality above, `km_distance` and `tan_div_pos`, none of this is proved here. For `pow` and `pown`, `pow_dir_exact` and `pown_dir_exact` show that they return, with the `f64` itself, at every finite argument whose exact result is an `f64`, with `x > 0` for x^y and `x` other than 0 for x^n. There, the rules for `y = ±0` and `x = 1` of x^y and for `n = 0` of x^n return 1. At the other such arguments, no rule of overflow, underflow or a result near 1 applies (`pow_rule_not_exact` and `pown_rule_not_exact`): `exact` returns x^y before the loop (`exact_complete`), and `ev_pown` gives the point |x|^n at every level, which `ziv` returns where the fast path has not returned it already (`fast_pow_dir_correct`), and `pown` negates for an odd `n` and `x < 0` (`pown_exact_odd`, `ev_pown_exact` and `ziv_exact`). At the other finite arguments that neither a rule nor the fast path settles, it would take that the enclosures are narrow enough by 4,096 bits, and that no `assert!`, such as those of `to_k` and of `scaled`, and no operation of the kernel panics, none of which is proved here. The elementary functions of `src/elementary.rs` other than `cbrt` and `hypot` compute their bounds with these functions of the kernel, through `src/mpfr_calls/kernel.rs`, so neither is it proved of them: the theorems of `DyPoint.v` and `DyPointDec.v` and the constructors `made_kernel_<f>` and `dmade_kernel_<f>` of `Valid.v` cover their calls at which each call of the kernel returns by a rule or a passing rounding test, and not the calls at which one panics or reaches the cap, where those of `DyPointSide.v` show only that the intervals of `Interval` contain the range (see the next two bullets).
- That the value of `ziv` at its cap, where no rounding test passes, is within one `f64` of the correct rounding. A proof would need, for each function, a bound on the width of its enclosure at 4,096 bits, such as one below the gap between the `f64`s around its result. No known argument reaches the cap. The checks of `ziv_cap` in the `levels.rs` tests of each function show only that, where the test passes at 64 limbs, it returns the rounding, as `ziv_at` does. The step at the cap where no test passes is tested on an enclosure of [0, 1] at every level (`ziv_rounds_outward_at_the_cap` in `src/dyadic/tests.rs`), and on the functions of the kernel by `check_widened` there, which widens their enclosures to contain the two `f64`s around the value, so that no test passes, and checks that `ziv` returns the rounding at the arguments of the `levels.rs` tests. That shows only that the enclosure at 64 limbs lies between those two `f64`s at those arguments.
- That the elementary functions return the tightest intervals at an argument at which a call of the kernel reaches the cap of `ziv` without a test passing: `kernel_exp_encloses` and the others of `DyPointSide.v` show only that the intervals of `Interval` contain the range there. Nor that the decorated elementary functions contain the range there and take the decorations that IEEE 1788 asks for: the theorems of `DyPointDec.v` and the constructors `made_kernel_<f>` and `dmade_kernel_<f>` of `Valid.v` take `kernel_point`, whose fields are the result rounded by definition there (see "What is assumed"). Nothing is proved of the elementary functions at an argument at which a call of the kernel panics, where `kernel_point_rust` takes the result rounded by definition too.
- That the functions of the kernel rounded to nearest, `exp_near` and the others of `src/dyadic/*.rs`, and with them the `_rn` functions of `inari::cr`, return the rounding to nearest, that is, that a rounding test of `ziv_near` passes by its cap of 4,096 bits: no level is shown to pass, and where none does, `ziv_near_cap_round` shows only that the result is the rounding to nearest of a bound of the enclosure at 64 limbs, not that it is one of the two `f64`s next to the number enclosed. Where that number is the midpoint of two adjacent `f64`s, the two bounds of an enclosure that holds it strictly inside round to different `f64`s, so it would also take, at each argument that no rule settles, that the result is not such a midpoint or that it is a bound of an enclosure, as in an exact one. For x^y at `x > 0`, `pow_near_midpoint` shows that `pow_near` settles every midpoint, by `exact_near`, or by the fast path where that returns first. That 2^-1075, which `exp2_near` gives at -1075, and x^n at a midpoint, which `pown_near` gives, have exact enclosures, so that a test of `ziv_near` passes, is not proved, nor that 23, where `exp10_near` has a rule, is the one integer at which 10^x is a midpoint. The tests check the values there (`near_exact_values` in `src/dyadic/exp/tests.rs`, and `odd_powers` and `subnormal_midpoints` in `src/dyadic/pow/tests/near.rs`), which the step at the cap would also give where the two bounds round to the two `f64`s next to the midpoint, as it takes the even one. A midpoint is rational too, so the facts on which `exp_dir_exact` and the others rest would apply: that log2 x and log10 x are not midpoints follows from `pow2_rational` and `pow10_rational`, as for `f64`s, as does that 2^x and 10^x at a non-integer `f64` x are not, and that the results of the other functions are not midpoints where they are not `f64`s follows from `exp_irrational` for e^x, tanh x, ln x and atanh x, and from `cos_irrational` for the trigonometric and inverse trigonometric functions, but for `sinh`, `cosh`, `asinh` and `acosh`, for which it takes `exp_not_quadratic`, as a rational sinh r or cosh r would make e^r a root of a quadratic with rational coefficients. None of this is stated for midpoints here. None of it concerns `cbrt_rn`, `hypot_rn` and `sqrt_rn`, which do not call `ziv_near` and whose theorems hold at every finite argument (`x >= 0` for `sqrt_rn`).
- The values of the functions rounded to nearest and of those of `inari::cr` in all three roundings at infinite and NaN arguments and at finite ones outside the domains of their theorems, such as `ln_rn(0.0)`, which is -∞, and those of `pow_rd`, `pow_ru` and `pow_rn` at `x = -∞` and, at a finite `x < 0` and a finite `y` that is not an integer, where they are NaN, and at a finite `x < 0` and an infinite `y`; and the sign of a zero that they return, which `mpfr_round`, the contract of the directed functions, does not state, and `near_round` does not state where the exact result is 0, such as `sin_rn(-0.0)` and `sin_rd(-0.0)`, which are -0: the tests compare them with MPFR on 64-bit Linux and macOS, but no theorem states them. The theorems above do state them for `cbrt`, `hypot` and `sqrt` of `inari::cr` in all three roundings, at every argument (`cbrt_special`, `hypot_inf`, `hypot_nan`, `hypot_zeros`, `cr_sqrt_zero`, `cr_sqrt_inf` and `cr_sqrt_nan`), and these return a zero only at a zero, as their exact results at the other finite arguments are at least 2^-1074 in magnitude, `sqrt_rd` and `sqrt_ru` as the portable backend computes them.
- The `nom` grammar of `src/parse.rs`, the first step of `FromStr` for intervals and decorated intervals, `interval!(s)`, `dec_interval!(s)` and `interval_exact!`, which reads an interval literal and computes its bounds as `Rat`s, exact rationals `(-1)^neg * num * 2^e2 * 5^e5 / den`. The ITF1788 tests in `tests/itf1788_tests` and the tests in `src/parse.rs` test it. That it returns only `NInterval`s with `ninterval_ok`, which the theorems about `From<NInterval>` and `From<DecNInterval>` assume, and so with `number_ok` of both bounds, which the theorem about `try_from_ninterval_exact` assumes, is checked by reading. The `DecNInterval`s that it returns then have `dec_ninterval_ok`, which the theorem about `From<DecNInterval>` assumes, by `dn_NAI_ok`, `dn_new_ok` and `dn_decorated_ok` for the step that decorates a literal, and by reading for the `nom` combinators that pass each literal to that step (see "What is assumed").
- `Nat::from_dec`, `Nat::from_hex` and `-` of `src/nat.rs`, with which the grammar computes the bounds of a literal, and `Nat::to_dec`, with which `src/format.rs` writes the decimal digits, are not modelled. That the first three return no zero limb at the top is checked by reading (see "What is assumed"), and the tests in `nat.rs` compare them and the other operations of `Nat` with `rug::Integer`. Nor is it proved that `impl Ord for Rat` in `src/parse/rat.rs`, which `number_le` takes as the order of the values of two `Rat`s, compares their values: the tests in `rat.rs` compare it with `rug::Rational` for small exponents, and for exponents up to `i32::MAX` in magnitude with bounds that MPFR computes, where those decide the order.
- The text that `Display`, `LowerExp` and `LowerHex` of intervals and decorated intervals write, in `fmt_impl`, `bound`, `fixed_text`, `sci_text` and `hex_text` of `src/format.rs`, apart from the digits that the functions of `src/format/digits.rs` compute: that `fmt_impl` passes `up = false` for the lower bound and `up = true` for the upper one, which is checked by reading, and the text that these functions write from the digits `d` and the exponent `k`, with the decimal digits that `Nat::to_dec` gives: the point, the zeros, the exponent, the digits of a rounding that carries into a new digit, the minus sign, which `bound` writes for every negative bound, -0 included, the text of 0 and of the infinities, which they write without `src/format/digits.rs`, that of the empty interval, the entire interval and NaI, the decoration and the width. The tests in `src/format/diff_tests.rs` compare the text of each bound, in both directions, with that of `fmt_bound_mpfr` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which only the tests compile, and which prints it with `mpfr::asprintf` as inari did before: for random bit patterns, integers, numbers beside a decimal carry or with hex digits at an edge, and other edge cases, each also negated, without a precision and with the precisions 0 to 20, and for some of them 40, 100, 330, 1100 and 65535. They compare `format!` of random intervals and decorated intervals, with and without a width and a precision, with the bounds of MPFR in the layout of `fmt_impl`, and parse the text back: it parses, contains the interval and keeps the decoration, or gives `dac` for `com` where the text of a bound is beyond `f64::MAX`, and `{:x}` without a precision gives the interval back exactly. In these tests, MPFR's text differs only for `{:.0x}` of a positive upper bound or a negative lower bound of at most 4 significant bits, where the comparison of bounds asks that the texts differ and that of whole intervals skips them (see "What is proved"). The test `format` in `src/format.rs` checks the text of some intervals.
- That the proofs hold on 32-bit targets, on which the library also builds and its tests run (i686 Linux in `ci-cross.yml`): `Model.v` models `usize` as `u64`, as on 64-bit targets. Two of its transcriptions say so: `<<` of `src/nat.rs`, whose `usize::try_from(k / 64).unwrap()` it takes to hold, though on a 32-bit target it panics if `k / 64` is at least 2^32, and the section on `src/format/digits.rs`. That no caller of `<<` shifts so far is checked by reading only: `scale` and the `<< 1` of `uncertain_interval` in `src/parse.rs`, and `cmp_shifted` and `pow5_bound` in `src/parse/rat.rs`, shift by at most 56 more than the bits of a `Nat` that they already hold, `pow10` by less than 2^32, after its `u32::try_from(k).unwrap()`, and `exact_dec` and `cut_dec` in `src/format/digits.rs` by at most 1,074. `fixed`, `sci` and `hex` there use their `usize` precision `p` only in `p.min(ALL_DIGITS) as i64` and, for `p < 13`, `p as u32`, whose values do not depend on the width of `usize`, and `format_fixed_correct` and `format_sci_correct` hold for every `p` below 2^64.
- Every other operation, such as `mid` of intervals.

## Building

You need Rocq 9.1, its Stdlib, Flocq 4.2.2, Gappa 1.6.0 and its tactic for Rocq, coq-gappa 1.11.0. Gappa builds against MPFR and Boost, which opam takes from the system (its packages `conf-mpfr` and `conf-boost`), on Debian and Ubuntu from `libmpfr-dev` and `libboost-dev`. With [opam](https://opam.ocaml.org):

```bash
opam repo add rocq-released https://coq.inria.fr/opam/released
opam install rocq-core.9.1.1 rocq-stdlib.9.0.0 coq-flocq.4.2.2 gappa.1.6.0 coq-gappa.1.11.0
make -C rocq
```

You can also use Docker, as CI does ([`ci-rocq.yml`](../.github/workflows/ci-rocq.yml)). The container runs as root, as apt needs to install MPFR and Boost, so the files that it writes in `rocq/` belong to root. `OPAMROOT` names the opam root of the image's user, `rocq`, and `OPAMROOTISOK` stops opam from warning that it runs as root.

```bash
docker run --rm --user root -e OPAMROOT=/home/rocq/.opam -e OPAMROOTISOK=1 \
  -v "$PWD/rocq:/w" -w /w rocq/rocq-prover:9.1 \
  bash -c 'apt-get update -qq && apt-get install -y -qq libmpfr-dev libboost-dev &&
    opam install -y coq-flocq.4.2.2 gappa.1.6.0 coq-gappa.1.11.0 && make'
```

Warnings are errors. The `Makefile` runs its own recipes, though not those of the `RocqMakefile` that it generates, under bash with `-euo pipefail`; these flags take GNU Make 3.82 or later, and an older GNU Make runs the recipes under bash without them. `make clean` removes the build outputs.
